Strichartz estimates and low regularity solutions of 3D relativistic Euler equations
Huali Zhang

Abstract
. We study the low regularity well-posedness for Cauchy problem of 3D relativistic Euler equations. Firstly, we introduce a new decomposition for relativistic velocity and derive new transport equations for vorticity, which both play a crucial role in energy and Strichartz estimates. According to Smith-Tataru’s approach, we then establish a Strichartz estimate of linear wave equations endowed with the acoustic metric. This leads us to prove a complete local well-posedness result if the initial logarithmic enthalpy, velocity, and modified vorticity ( h 0 , 𝐮 0 , 𝐰 0 ) ∈ H s × H s × H s 0 ​ ( 2 < s 0 < s ) (h_{0},\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{s}\times H^{s}\times H^{s_{0}}(2<s_{0}<s) . Therefore, we give an affirmative answer to ”Open Problem D” proposed by Disconzi. Moreover, for ( h 0 , 𝐮 0 , 𝐰 0 ) ∈ H 2 + × H 2 + × H 2 (h_{0},{\mathbf{u}}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{2+}\times H^{2+}\times H^{2} , by frequency truncation, there is a stronger Strichartz estimate for solutions on a short-time-interval. By semi-classical analysis and induction method, these solutions can be extended from short time intervals to a regular time interval, and a uniform Strichartz estimate with loss of derivatives can be obtained. This allows us to prove the local well-posedness of 3D relativistic equations if ( h 0 , 𝐮 0 , 𝐰 0 ) ∈ H 2 + × H 2 + × H 2 (h_{0},{\mathbf{u}}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{2+}\times H^{2+}\times H^{2} .



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 35A01, 35Q75, 35R05

1.
Introduction

In the paper, we study the low regularity well-posedness theory for the Cauchy problem of relativistic Euler equations in the Minkowski background
\mathbb{R}^{1+3}
. The relativistic Euler equation describes the motion of a relativistic fluid, which is an essential model in mathematical general relativity. To start our problem, let us introduce the formulation of relativistic Euler equations in mathematics.

The fluid state is represented by the energy density
\varrho
, and the relativistic velocity
\mathbf{u}=(u^{0},u^{1},u^{2},u^{3})^{\textrm{T}}
. The velocity is assumed to be a forward time-like vector field, normalized by
1
1

1

We also use the Greek indices
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\cdots
take on the values
0,1,2,3
, while the Latin spatial indices
a,b,c,\cdots,i,j,k,\cdots
take on the values
1,2,3
. Repeated indices are summed over (from
0
to
3
if they are Greek, and from
1
to
3
if they are Latin). Greek and Latin indices are lowered and raised with the Minkowski metric
\mathbf{m}
and its inverse
\mathbf{m}^{-1}
. We will use this convention throughout the paper unless otherwise indicated.

u^{\alpha}u_{\alpha}=-1,

(1.1)

where
u_{\alpha}=m_{\alpha\beta}u^{\beta}
, and
\mathbf{m}=(m_{\alpha\beta})_{4\times 4}
is the Minkowski metric. Due to the constraint (
1.1
), so there only three of the quantities
u^{1},u^{2},u^{3}

are independent. We denote

\mathring{\mathbf{u}}=(u^{1},u^{2},u^{3}).

(1.2)

Therefore, we can also write the velocity as

\mathbf{u}=(u^{0},\mathring{\mathbf{u}})^{\mathrm{T}}.

(1.3)

An appropriate equation of state (please c.f. Rendall
[
46
]
) is of the form

p=p(\varrho)=\varrho^{\vartheta},\quad\mathrm{where}\ \vartheta>1\ \mathrm{is}\ \mathrm{a}\ \mathrm{constant}.

(1.4)

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the stress energy tensor, which is defined by

\mathcal{Q}^{\alpha\beta}=(p+\varrho)u^{\alpha}u^{\beta}+pm^{\alpha\beta}.

Then the motion of the relativistic Euler equations can be described by

\partial_{\alpha}\mathcal{Q}^{\alpha\beta}=0.

(1.5)

Projecting (
1.5
) into the subspace
parallel and orthogonal to
u^{\alpha}
yields (please c.f. Disconzi-Ifrim-Tataru
[
17
]
or Oliynyk
[
44
,
45
]
)

\begin{cases}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}\varrho+(p+\varrho)\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa}=0,\\
(p+\varrho)u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\alpha}+(m^{\alpha\kappa}+u^{\alpha}u^{\kappa})\partial_{\kappa}p=0.\end{cases}

(1.6)

To study the Strichartz estimate and low regularity solutions, a good start is to see the specific structure of (
1.6
), ie. a wave-transport system
[
19
]
. To get the wave-transport structure, we first introduce some good variables as follows.

1.1.
Definitions and good variables

Let us introduce some variables–acoustic speed, number density, enthalpy, and the modified vorticity.

Definition 1.1
.

[
19
]

We denote the acoustic speed
c_{s}

c_{s}=\sqrt{\frac{dp}{d\varrho}},

(1.7)

the number density
q

p+\varrho=q\frac{d\varrho}{dq},

(1.8)

and the enthalpy per particle
H

H=\frac{p+\varrho}{q}.

(1.9)

The vanishing of
c_{s}
causes a degeneracy in the system, so we restrict our attention to solution in which

0<c_{s}\leq 1.

(1.10)

Definition 1.2
.

[
19
]

Let
\bar{H}>0
be a fixed constant value of the enthalpy. We denote the (dimensionless) logarithmic enthalpy
h

h=\log\frac{H}{\bar{H}}.

To be simple, we assume
\bar{H}=1
. Then we have

h=\log{H}.

(1.11)

Under the settings (
1.7
), (
1.8
), (
1.9
), and (
1.11
), then
\varrho,p
, and
c_{s}
are functions of
h
. To be simple, we record

\varrho=\varrho(h),\quad p=p(h),\quad c_{s}=c_{s}(h).

(1.12)

In terms of these variables, after calculations (please refer Lemma
9.1
below), the relativistic Euler equations (
1.6
) becomes

\begin{cases}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h=-c^{2}_{s}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa},\\
u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\alpha}=-(m^{\alpha\kappa}+u^{\alpha}u^{\kappa})\partial_{\kappa}h.\end{cases}

(1.13)

Definition 1.3
.

Let
H
and
h
be stated in (
1.9
)- (
1.11
). For any given one-form
\mathbf{A}=(A^{0},A^{1},A^{2},A^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
, define the
\mathbf{u}
-vorticity
2
2

2

In relativistic fluids, the vorticity
w_{\alpha\beta}
is defined by
w_{\alpha\beta}=\partial_{\alpha}u_{\beta}-\partial_{\beta}u_{\alpha}
(cf. Choquet-Bruhat
[
13
]
). Our purpose here is to find a good structure of the
\mathbf{u}
-vorticity and its higher derivatives, please see Lemma
2.15
and Lemma
2.17
.

\mathrm{vort}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{A})=-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}A_{\delta},

(1.14)

where
\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
is the fully antisymmetric symbol normalized by
\epsilon_{0123}=1
. We also denote the modified vorticity
\mathbf{w}=(w^{0},w^{1},w^{2},w^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
by

{w}^{\alpha}=\mathrm{vort}^{\alpha}(H\mathbf{u})=-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}(Hu_{\delta})=-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\mathrm{e}^{h}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\delta}.

(1.15)

Definition 1.4
.

We define the acoustical metric
g=(g_{\alpha\beta})_{4\times 4}
and its inverse
g^{-1}=(g^{\alpha\beta})_{4\times 4}
as
3
3

3

In this setting,
g
is a Lorentz metric and
g^{00}=-1
.

\begin{split}g_{\alpha\beta}&=\Omega^{-1}\left(c_{s}^{-2}m_{\alpha\beta}+(c_{s}^{-2}-1)u_{\alpha}u_{\beta}\right),\\
g^{\alpha\beta}&=\Omega\left(c_{s}^{2}m^{\alpha\beta}+(c_{s}^{2}-1)u^{\alpha}u^{\beta}\right),\end{split}

(1.16)

where
\Omega
is defined by

\begin{split}\Omega=\left(c_{s}^{2}+(1-c_{s}^{2})(u^{0})^{2}\right)^{-1}.\end{split}

(1.17)

Definition 1.5
.

Let
\mathbf{u}
be the relativistic velocity and
h
the logarithmic enthalpy. Let
\mathbf{w}
be set in (
1.15
). We denote the fluid variables
\mathbf{W}=(W^{0},W^{1},W^{2},W^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
and
\mathbf{G}=(G^{0},G^{1},G^{2},G^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
by

\begin{split}W^{\alpha}=&\mathrm{vort}^{\alpha}(\mathrm{e}^{h}\mathbf{w})=-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}w_{\delta}+c_{s}^{-2}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}w_{\delta}\partial_{\gamma}h,\\
G^{\alpha}=&\mathrm{vort}^{\alpha}\mathbf{W}=-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}W_{\delta}.\end{split}

(1.18)

Definition 1.6
.

Let
\mathbf{u}
be the relativistic velocity and
h
the logarithmic enthalpy. Let
\mathbf{w},\mathbf{W}
and
\mathbf{G}
be defined in (
1.15
) and (
1.18
). We denote

\mathring{\mathbf{w}}=(w^{1},w^{2},w^{3})^{\mathrm{T}},\quad\mathring{\mathbf{W}}=(W^{1},W^{2},W^{3})^{\mathrm{T}},\quad\mathring{\mathbf{G}}=(G^{1},G^{2},G^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}.

(1.19)

We are now ready to introduce a wave-transport system of (
1.13
).

1.2.
Wave-transport system

Lemma 1.1
.

Let
(h,\mathbf{u})
be a solution of (
1.13
). Let
\mathbf{w}
and
\mathbf{W}
be defined in (
1.15
) and (
1.18
). Then System (
1.13
) can be written as

\begin{cases}\square_{g}h=D,\\
\square_{g}u^{\alpha}=-c_{s}^{2}\Omega\mathrm{e}^{-h}W^{\alpha}+Q^{\alpha},\\
u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}w^{\alpha}=-u^{\alpha}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h+w^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\alpha}-w^{\alpha}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa},\\
\partial_{\alpha}w^{\alpha}=-w^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h,\end{cases}

(1.20)

where the operator
\square_{g}=g^{\beta\gamma}\partial^{2}_{\beta\gamma}
, quantities
D
, and
Q^{\alpha}
are respectively given by

D=\Omega\left\{(1-c^{2}_{s})u^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h+u^{\beta}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\beta}(c^{2}_{s})-c^{2}_{s}u^{\beta}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\beta}h-c^{2}_{s}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\right\},

(1.21)

and

\begin{split}Q^{\alpha}=&\Omega\big\{u^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\alpha}+u^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}(m^{\alpha\kappa}+u^{\alpha}u^{\kappa})\partial_{\kappa}h-(m^{\alpha\kappa}+u^{\alpha}u^{\kappa})\partial_{\beta}u^{\beta}\partial_{\kappa}(c^{2}_{s})\\
&-(m^{\alpha\kappa}+u^{\alpha}u^{\kappa})\partial_{\kappa}u^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}h+c^{2}_{s}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\mathrm{e}^{-h}w_{\delta}\partial_{\beta}u_{\gamma}+c^{2}_{s}\partial_{\beta}u^{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}h-c^{2}_{s}\partial^{\beta}u^{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}h\\
&+c^{2}_{s}u^{\alpha}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\beta}u^{\beta}\partial_{\kappa}h+c^{2}_{s}u^{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\beta}-c^{2}_{s}u^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\alpha}-c^{2}_{s}u^{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\beta}u^{\alpha}\partial_{\kappa}h\big\}.\end{split}

(1.22)

Remark 1.1
.

The system (
1.20
) is first proposed by Disconzi-Speck
[
19
]
(please see Theorem 3.1 and set
S=0
). We also give a derivation for it, i.e. Lemma
1.1
in Appendix
9.1
.

Remark 1.2
.

For brevity, we set
\mathbf{Q}=(Q^{0},Q^{1},Q^{2},Q^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
. By (
1.21
) and (
1.22
), we can also write
Q^{\alpha}=Q_{1\kappa}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\partial_{\beta}h\partial_{\gamma}u^{\kappa}+Q_{2\kappa\delta}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\partial_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\delta}+Q_{3}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\partial_{\beta}h\partial_{\gamma}h
,
D=D_{1\kappa}^{\beta\gamma}\partial_{\beta}h\partial_{\gamma}u^{\kappa}+D_{2\kappa\delta}^{\beta\gamma}\partial_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\delta}+D_{3}^{\beta\gamma}\partial_{\beta}h\partial_{\gamma}h
. Here
Q_{1\kappa}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}
,
Q_{2\kappa\delta}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}
,
Q_{3}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}
,
D_{1\kappa}^{\beta\gamma}
,
D_{2\kappa\delta}^{\beta\gamma}
, and
D_{3}^{\beta\gamma}
are smooth functions of
\mathbf{u}
and
h
, and they are uniquely determined by (
1.22
).

Remark 1.3
.

Under the normalized condition (
1.9
) and (
1.11
), the system (
1.20
) is derived from (
1.13
), with (
1.15
)-(
1.18
), and using this convention, the systems (
1.6
), (
1.13
), and (
1.20
), as well as (
2.12
) below, are equivalent.

Seeing (
1.20
), we also need a decomposition for the velocity as follows.

Definition 1.7
.

Let
\mathbf{u}
be the relativistic velocity and
h
the logarithmic enthalpy. Let
\mathbf{w}
and
\mathbf{W}
be defined in (
1.15
) and (
1.18
). Set
\mathrm{I}
be the identity operator. Introduce a composition of
\mathbf{u}
by

u^{\alpha}=u^{\alpha}_{+}+u^{\alpha}_{-},

(1.23)

where
\mathbf{u}_{-}=(u_{-}^{0},u_{-}^{1},u_{-}^{2},u_{-}^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
is denoted by

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}u_{-}^{\alpha}&=\mathrm{e}^{-h}W^{\alpha},\quad\mathbf{P}=\mathrm{I}-(m^{\beta\gamma}+2u^{\beta}u^{\gamma})\partial^{2}_{\beta\gamma}.\end{split}

(1.24)

Remark 1.4
.

(1)

By (
1.24
) and (
1.1
), then
\mathbf{P}
is a space-time elliptic operator on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. For any function
f
defined on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, by a simple extension technique, we can derive that

\|f\|_{L_{[0,T]}^{2}H_{x}^{a}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{P}f\|_{L_{[0,T]}^{2}H_{x}^{a-2}},\quad a\in\mathbb{R}.

(1.25)

This will be proved in Lemma
9.2
below. Therefore, we can record
\mathbf{P}^{-1}
as the inverse operator for
\mathbf{P}
, and also

\begin{split}u^{\alpha}_{-}=&\mathbf{P}^{-1}(\mathrm{e}^{-h}W^{\alpha}).\end{split}

(1.26)

(2)

This construction for
\mathbf{P}
is new in the relativistic Euler equations. For non-relativistic compressible Euler equations, referring the work
[
56
,
8
]
,
\mathbf{P}
is the space-elliptic operator
\mathrm{I}-\Delta
(
\Delta=\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2}+\partial_{3}^{2}
). While, in relativistic Euler equations, if we take
\mathbf{P}
as a space-elliptic operator, it doesn’t work. We make a short explanation as follows.

If
\mathbf{P}
is a space-elliptic elliptic operator, then

\begin{split}\square_{g}\mathbf{u}_{+}=&\square_{{g}}\mathbf{u}-\square_{g}\mathbf{u}_{-}\\
=&\underbrace{-c_{s}^{2}\Omega\mathrm{e}^{-h}W^{\alpha}-\Omega c_{s}^{2}m^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{u}_{-}}_{=\psi}-\Omega(c_{s}^{2}-1)u^{\alpha}u^{\beta}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{u}_{-}+\mathrm{l.o.t.},\end{split}

For
\mathbf{u}\in H^{s_{0}+1}
, then
d^{2}\mathbf{u}\in H^{s_{0}-1}
and
\mathbf{u}_{+}\in H^{s_{0}}
. This implies
\mathbf{u}\in H^{s_{0}}
, which has
(s-s_{0})
-order loss of derivatives.

If
\mathbf{P}
is defined as in (
1.24
), then
\psi\approx u^{\alpha}u^{\beta}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{u}_{-}
. Moreover, we can calculate (see Lemma
2.18
below)

u^{\alpha}u^{\beta}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{u}_{-}\approx g^{0\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{u}_{-}+\nabla\mathbf{T}\mathbf{u}_{-}+\mathrm{l.o.t.}

By Duhamel’s principles, we only need to check if
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{u}_{-}\in L^{2}_{t}H^{s}_{x}
holds or not. Very fortunately, combining (
1.24
)-(
1.25
), together with the transport equation for
\mathbf{W}
, we can prove
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{u}_{-}\in L^{2}_{t}H^{s}_{x}
. Therefore, we have
\mathbf{u}_{+}\in H^{s}
, and then
\mathbf{u}\in H^{s}
. In a word, there is no loss of derivatives if we define
\mathbf{P}
as in (
1.24
).

Our goal in this paper is to give a complete well-posedness result of rough solutions for the Cauchy problem of (
1.13
), and answer the open question ”Problem D” proposed by Disconzi
[
16
]
. Set the initial data

(h,\mathbf{u})|_{t=0}=(h_{0},\mathbf{u}_{0}),\qquad\mathbf{u}_{0}=(u_{0}^{0},u_{0}^{1},u_{0}^{2},u_{0}^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}.

(1.27)

By (
1.1
),
\mathbf{u}_{0}
should satisfy

u^{\alpha}_{0}u_{0\alpha}=-1,

(1.28)

where

u_{0\alpha}=m_{\alpha\beta}u^{\beta}_{0}.

(1.29)

1.3.
History and motivation

In the non-relativistic case, namely, compressible Euler equations, the local well-posedness result began with Majda’s work
[
37
]
. In
[
37
]
, the initial velocity, density and entropy belong to
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),s>1+\frac{n}{2}
and the density bounded away from vacuum. If the flow is isentropic and irrotational, the compressible Euler equations can be written as a quasilinear wave system. A general quasilinear wave equation takes the form

\begin{cases}&\square_{h(\phi)}\phi=q(d\phi,d\phi),\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
&(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)|_{t=0}=(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\end{cases}

(1.30)

where
\phi
is a scalar function,
h(\phi)
is a Lorentzian metric depending on
\phi
,
d=(\partial_{t},\partial_{1},\partial_{2},\cdots,\partial_{n})
, and
q
is quadratic in
d\phi
. According to classical energy methods and Sobolev imbeddings, Hughes-Kato-Marsden
[
23
]
proved the local well-posedness of the problem (
1.30
) for
s>\frac{n}{2}+1
. On the other side, Lindblad
[
35
]
constructed some counterexamples for (
1.30
) when
s=2,n=3
. For
s={\frac{7}{4}},n=2
, a 2D counterexample was constructed by Ohlman
[
43
]
. There is a gap between the result
[
23
]
and
[
35
,
43
]
. To lower the regularity of the initial data, a classical idea is to prove Strichartz estimates of solutions. There are several steps to obtain the sharp Strichartz estimates for (
1.30
). The first step is to consider the the wave equation with variable coefficients

\square_{h(t,x)}\phi=0,

(1.31)

and then exploit it to obtain the low regularity solutions of (
1.30
). For smooth coefficients
h
, Kapitanskij
[
27
]
and Mockenhaupt-Seeger-Sogge
[
41
]
both proved the Strichartz estimates for (
1.31
). For rough coefficients
h
, Smith
[
47
]
firstly proved the Strichartz eatimate for
h\in C^{2}
in two or three dimensions. Inspired by
[
47
]
, Tataru
[
52
]
established the Strichartz estimates in all dimensions. However, for
h\in C^{\alpha}
(
0<\alpha<2
), the Strichartz estimates fails, one can see the counterexample constructed by Smith-Sogge
[
48
]
.

The second step was independently achieved by Bahouri-Chemin
[
11
]
and Tataru
[
51
]
, who established the local well-posedness of (
1.30
) with
s>\frac{n}{2}+\frac{7}{8},n=2
or
s>\frac{n}{2}+\frac{3}{4},n\geq 3
. Shortly afterward, Tataru
[
53
]
relaxed the Sobolev indices
s>\frac{n+1}{2}+\frac{1}{6},n\geq 3
. Additionally, Smith-Tataru
[
49
]
showed that the
\frac{1}{6}
loss is sharp for general variable coefficients
h
. Thus, to improve the above results, one needs to exploit a new way or structure of Equation (
1.30
).

By finding a good decomposition of curvature and vector-field approach, Klainerman-Rodnianski
[
31
]
realized the next key progress in 3D rough solutions of (
1.30
) with
s>2+\frac{2-\sqrt{3}}{2}
.
Based on
[
31
]
, Geba
[
26
]
also studied the local well-posedness of (
1.30
) in two dimensions for
s>\frac{7}{4}+\frac{5-\sqrt{22}}{4}
. By using wave packets of localization to represent solutions of a linear equation, a sharp result was proved by Smith-Tataru
[
50
]
, who established the local well-posedness of (
1.30
) if
s>\frac{7}{4},n=2
or
s>2,n=3
or
s>\frac{n+1}{2},4\leq n\leq 5
. An alternative proof of the 3D case in
[
50
]
was also obtained through a vector-field approach by Wang
[
55
]
.

There are also other two important quasilinear wave models, namely, Einstein vacuum and time-like minimal surface equations. In the aspect of Einstein vacuum equations, the results depend on the choice of gauge. In Yang-Mills gauge, Klainerman-Rodnianski-Szeftel
[
33
]
solved the well-known
L^{2}
conjecture. In wave gauge, the equation is well-posed in
H^{2+}
and ill-posed in
H^{2}
, cf. Klainerman-Rodnianski
[
32
]
and Ettinger-Lindblad
[
20
]
. In CMC guage, the system is well-posed in
H^{2+}
, please refer Andersson-Moncrief
[
7
]
and Q. Wang
[
54
]
. For the time-like minimal surface equations, which satisfy null conditions, Ai-Ifrim-Tataru
[
2
]
obtained a substantial improvement, namely by
\frac{1}{8}
derivatives in two space dimensions and by
\frac{1}{4}
derivatives in higher dimensions.

If the flow is rotational or isentropic, the compressible Euler equations can be written as a coupled wave-transport system, which is proposed by Luk-Speck
[
36
]
. Based on Luk-Speck’s work, the first study of rough solutions was independently obtained by Disconzi-Luo-Mazzone-Speck
[
18
]
and Wang
[
56
]
. Disconzi-Luo-Mazzone-Speck
[
18
]
proved the existence and uniqueness of solutions of compressible Euler equations if
4
4

4

Here
S_{0}
is the initial entropy.

(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0},S_{0})\in H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{3+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with an additional Hölder condition
\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}_{0},\Delta S_{0}\in C^{\sigma},0<\sigma<1
. Wang
[
56
]
established the existence and uniqueness of solutions of isentropic compressible Euler equations if
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, where
2<s_{0}<s
. The work
[
18
]
and
[
56
]
both are based on classical vector-fields approach. In a different view, using Smith-Tataru’s method
[
50
]
, together with semi-classical analysis and Tao’s frequency envelope, Andersson-Zhang
[
8
]
proved a complete local well-posedness of 3D compressible Euler equations with relaxed assumptions
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
or
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0},S_{0})\in H^{\frac{5}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{\frac{5}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{\frac{3}{2}+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{\frac{5}{2}+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. We should also mention some results in two dimensions. Zhang
[
62
,
63
]
proved the well-posedness of solutions of 2D isentropic compressible Euler equations when
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\nabla\mathbf{w}_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
or
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. In the reverse direction, there are two ill-posedness results if
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0})\in\dot{H}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
or
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0})\in\dot{H}^{\frac{7}{4}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with smooth vorticity, please refer the joint works by An-Chen-Yin
[
4
,
5
]
.

In the study of relativistic Euler equations, Makino-Ukai
[
38
,
39
]
proved the Cauchy problem of local well-posedness of the 3D relativistic Euler equations when
s\geq 3
by studying the symmetric hyperbolic structure. For an overview of the standard theory, we refer to the relevant chapter in Choquet-Bruhat’s book
[
13
]
. With vacuum, the pioneering result was obtained by Rendall
[
46
]
and Guo-Tahvildar-Zadeh
[
21
]
. By proposing a new symmetrization, LeFloch-Ukai
[
34
]
established a local-in-time existence result for solutions in
n
-D containing vacuum states when
s>1+\frac{n}{2}
. Recently, Disconzi-Speck
[
19
]
proposed a new formulation of covariant wave equations coupled to transport equations and two transport-div-curl systems for the 3D relativistic Euler equations. On the basis of
[
18
]
and
[
19
]
, Yu
[
61
]
proved the existence and uniqueness of rough solutions of 3D relativistic Euler equations if
(\mathbf{u}_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0},S_{0})\in H^{2+}\times H^{2+}\times H^{2+}\times H^{3+}
, and
\mathrm{vort}\mathbf{w}_{0},\Delta S_{0}\in C^{\sigma}(0<\sigma<1)
. Very recently, Disconzi
[
16
]
proposed some open problems, one is about establishing the well-posedness of low regularity solutions for the 3D relativistic Euler equations with
(h_{0},\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{s}\times H^{s}\times H^{s_{0}}(2<s_{0}<s)
. We note that there are also some well-posed results on free boundary problems due to Jang-LeFloch- Masmoudi
[
25
]
, Had
\check{\mathrm{z}}
i
\acute{\mathrm{c}}
-Shkoller-Speck
[
22
]
, Disconzi-Ifrim-Tataru
[
17
]
, and Miao-Shahshahani
[
40
]
and so on.

Strongly motivated by these insightful works, we further study the Hadamard well-posedness of rough solutions for 3D relativistic Euler equations, and give a definite answer to the open question ”Problem D” in
[
16
]
. Firstly, we prove the local well-posedness of solutions for
(h_{0},{\mathbf{u}}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{2+}\times H^{2+}\times H^{s_{0}}
(
2<s_{0}<s
). In the proof, we also obtain: (i) a Strichartz estimate of linear wave equations endowed with the acoustic metric, (ii) a type of Strichartz estimate for solutions with a regularity of the velocity
2+
, enthalpy
2+
, and modified vorticity
\frac{3}{2}+
. Secondly, taking
(h_{0},{\mathbf{u}}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{2+}\times H^{2+}\times H^{2}
, then a stronger Strichartz estimates on a short time-interval hold. By summing up these short time intervals to a regular time-interval, a Strichartz estimate with loss of derivatives can be obtained
5
5

5

This argument is introduced by Ai-Ifrim-Tataru
[
2
]
in the study of time-like minimal surfaces.
. So we prove the local well-posedness of solutions of 3D relativistic Euler equations if
(h_{0},{\mathbf{u}}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{2+}\times H^{2+}\times H^{2}
.

1.4.
Statement of result

Before state our results precisely, let us introduce some notations and functional spaces as follows. Let
\nabla=(\partial_{1},\partial_{2},\partial_{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
,
d=(\partial_{t},\partial_{1},\partial_{2},\partial_{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
,
\left<\xi\right>=(1+|\xi|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}},\ \xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Denote by
\left<\nabla\right>
the corresponding Bessel potential multiplier. Define the operator

\mathbf{T}=\frac{1}{u^{0}}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}=\partial_{t}+\frac{u^{i}}{u^{0}}\partial_{i}.

(1.32)

and

\Delta=\partial^{2}_{1}+\partial^{2}_{2}+\partial^{2}_{3},\quad\Lambda_{x}=(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Let
\zeta
be a smooth function with support in the shell
\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\frac{1}{2}\leq|\xi|\leq 2\}
. Here,
\xi
denotes the variable of the spatial Fourier transform. Let
\zeta
also satisfy the
condition
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\zeta(2^{k}\xi)=1
. Let
{P}_{j}
be the Littlewood-Paley operator with frequency
2^{j},j\in\mathbb{Z}
(cf. Bahouri-Chemin-Danchin
[
12
]
, page 78),

{P}_{j}f=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}x\cdot\xi}\zeta(2^{-j}\xi)\hat{f}(\xi)d\xi.

(1.33)

For
f\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, let

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{s}}=\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})},

with the homogeneous norm
\|f\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{s}}={\sum_{j\geq-1}}2^{2js}\|{P}_{j}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}
. We shall also make use of the homogenous Besov norm

\displaystyle\|f\|^{r}_{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,r}}={\sum_{j\geq-1}}2^{jsr}\|{P}_{j}f\|^{r}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

To avoid confusion, when the function
f
is related to both time and space variables, we use the notation
\|f\|_{H_{x}^{s}}=\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}
.

Assume there holds
6
6

6

In the paper,
c_{s}|_{t=0}>c_{0}
represents that it’s far away from vacuum.
c_{s}<1
is from (
1.10
).

|h_{0},u_{0}^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}}_{0}|\leq C_{0},\qquad u_{0}^{0}\geq 1,\qquad c_{0}<c_{s}|_{t=0}<1.

(1.34)

where
C_{0},c_{0}>0
are positive constants. In the following, constants
C
depending only on
C_{0},c_{0}
shall be called universal. Unless otherwise stated, all constants that appear are universal in this sense.
The notation
X\lesssim Y
means
X\leq CY
, where
C
is a universal constant, possibly depending on
C_{0},c_{0}
. Similarly, we write
X\simeq Y
when
C_{1}Y\leq X\leq C_{2}Y
, with
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
universal constants, and
X\ll Y
when
X\leq CY
for a sufficiently large constant
C
. The universal constant may change from line to line.

Let
s_{0}
and
s
satisfy
2<s_{0}<s\leq\frac{5}{2}
.
We also set

\delta\in(0,s-2),\quad\delta_{0}\in(0,s_{0}-2),\quad\delta_{1}=\frac{s-2}{40}.

(1.35)

We use three small parameters

0<\epsilon_{3}\ll\epsilon_{2}\ll\epsilon_{1}\ll\epsilon_{0}\ll 1.

(1.36)

Our first result is stated as follows.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
2<s_{0}<s\leq\frac{5}{2}
. Consider the Cauchy problem (
1.13
)-(
1.27
). Let
\mathbf{w}
be defined in (
1.15
).
For any given initial data
(h_{0},\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
satisfies (
1.28
), (
1.34
), and for any
M_{0}>0
such that
7
7

7

Throughout our paper,
\mathbf{u}\in H^{s}_{x}
means
\mathbf{u}-(1,0,0,0)^{\mathrm{T}}\in H^{s}_{x}
.

\|(h_{0},{\mathbf{u}}_{0})\|_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\|_{H^{s_{0}}}\leq M_{0},

(1.37)

where

\mathbf{w}_{0}=\mathrm{vort}(\mathrm{e}^{h_{0}}\mathbf{u}_{0}),

(1.38)

there exist positive constants
T>0

and
M_{1}>0
(
T
and
{M}_{1}
depends on
C_{0},c_{0},s,s_{0},{M}_{0}
) such that (
1.13
)-(
1.27
) has a unique solution
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})
on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Moreover,
the following statements hold.

(1)

The solutions
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})
satisfy the energy estimate

\begin{split}&\|(h,\mathbf{u})\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T]}H_{x}^{s_{0}}}\leq M_{1},\\
&\|(\partial_{t}{\mathbf{u}},\partial_{t}h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T]}H_{x}^{s-1}}+\|\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T]}H_{x}^{s_{0}-1}}\leq M_{1},\end{split}

(1.39)

and

\|h,u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,{T}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\leq 1+C_{0},\qquad u^{0}\geq 1.

(2)

The solution also satisfies the Strichartz estimate

\|(d\mathbf{u},dh,d\mathbf{u}_{+})\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T]}L_{x}^{\infty}}+\|d\mathbf{u},dh\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T]}\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}}+\|\nabla\mathbf{u}_{+}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T]}\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}}\leq M_{1},

(1.40)

where
\mathbf{u}_{+}
is denoted in (
1.23
)-(
1.24
).

(3)

For any
1\leq r\leq s+1
, and for each
t_{0}\in[0,T)
, the linear equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{g}f=0,\qquad(t,x)\in(t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&(f,\partial_{t}f)|_{t=t_{0}}=(f_{0},f_{1})\in H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{r-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\end{cases}

(1.41)

admits a solution
f\in C([0,T],H_{x}^{r})\times C^{1}([0,T],H_{x}^{r-1})
and the following estimates hold:

\|f\|_{L_{[0,T]}^{\infty}H_{x}^{r}}+\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L_{[0,T]}^{\infty}H_{x}^{r-1}}\leq C_{M_{0}}(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}).

Additionally, the following estimates hold, provided
k<r-1
,

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{k}f\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C_{M_{0}}(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}),

(1.42)

and the same estimates hold with
\left<\nabla\right>^{k}
replaced by
\left<\nabla\right>^{k-1}d
. Here,
C_{M_{0}}
is a constant depending on
C_{0},c_{0},s,s_{0}
and
M_{0}
.

(4)

the map from
(h_{0},{\mathbf{u}}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{s}\times H^{s}\times H^{s_{0}}
to
(h,{\mathbf{u}},\mathbf{w})(t,\cdot)\in C([0,T];H_{x}^{s}\times H_{x}^{s}\times H_{x}^{s_{0}})
is continuous.

Remark 1.5
.

Compared with Yu’s result
[
61
]
, there is no Hölder condition on
\mathrm{vort}\mathbf{w}
in Theorem
1.2
. Moreover, We provide the proof of Hadamard well-posedness for the relativistic Euler equations with rough data.

Our second result is as follows.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
2<s<\frac{5}{2}
. Let
\mathbf{w}
be defined in (
1.15
). Consider the Cauchy problem (
1.13
)-(
1.27
). Assume that (
1.34
) holds. For any
M_{*}>0
, if

\|h_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{u}_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{2}}\leq M_{*},

(1.43)

where

\mathbf{w}_{0}=\mathrm{vort}(\mathrm{e}^{h_{0}}\mathbf{u}_{0}),

(1.44)

then there exist positive constants
T^{*}>0
and
{M}_{2}>0
(
T^{*}
and
{M}_{2}
depends on
C_{0},c_{0},s,M_{*}
) such that (
1.13
)-(
1.27
) has a unique solution
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})
on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. To be precise,

(1)

the solution
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})
satisfies the energy estimate

\begin{split}&\|(h,{\mathbf{u}})\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H_{x}^{2}}\leq{M}_{2},\\
&\|(\partial_{t}{\mathbf{u}},\partial_{t}h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H_{x}^{s-1}}+\|\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H_{x}^{1}}\leq{M}_{2},\end{split}

(1.45)

and

\|h,u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}},h\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,{T}^{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\leq 2+C_{0},\qquad u^{0}\geq 1.

(1.46)

(2)

the solution
(h,\mathbf{u})
satisfies the Strichartz estimate

\|(d\mathbf{u},dh)\|_{L^{2}_{[0,{T}^{*}]}L_{x}^{\infty}}\leq{M}_{2}.

(1.47)

(3)

for any
\frac{s}{2}\leq r\leq 3
, and for each
t_{0}\in[0,T^{*}]
, the linear wave equation (
1.41
) has a unique solution on
[0,T^{*}]
. Moreover, for
a\leq r-\frac{s}{2}
, the solution satisfies

\begin{split}&\|\left<\nabla\right>^{a-1}d{f}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq{M}_{3}(\|{f}_{0}\|_{{H}_{x}^{r}}+\|{f}_{1}\|_{{H}_{x}^{r-1}}),\\
&\|{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H^{r}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H^{r-1}_{x}}\leq{M}_{3}(\|{f}_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|{f}_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}).\end{split}

(1.48)

Here
{M}_{3}>0
depends on
C_{0},c_{0},s,{M}_{*}
.

(4)

the map from
(h_{0},\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{s}\times H^{s}\times H^{2}
to
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})(t,\cdot)\in C([0,T^{*}];H_{x}^{s}\times H_{x}^{s}\times H_{x}^{2})
is continuous.

Remark 1.6
.

(1)

Controlling the regularity of velocity and density, some good structure of (
1.13
) may allow us lowering the regularity of the vorticity. In Theorem
1.3
, the regularity of vorticity is
2
. So it provides us
(s_{0}-2)
-order (
s_{0}>2
) regularity improvement of the vorticity compared with Theorem
1.2
.

(2)

Whether the results in Theorem
1.2
, and
1.3
are sharp remains a challenging problem. We may expect that the local well-posedness of solutions for (
1.13
) also hold when
(h_{0},\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{2+}\times H^{2+}\times H^{\frac{3}{2}+}
.

Remark 1.7
.

(1)

By comparison, (
1.42
) is better than (
1.48
). There is a Strichartz estimate with a loss of derivatives in (
1.42
), which is proved by extending the short (semi-classical) time-interval to a regular time-interval by summing up estimates on these short time-intervals. The idea is introduced by Ai-Ifrim-Tataru
[
2
]
in time-like minimal surfaces, and developed by Andersson-Zhang
[
8
]
in 3D compressible Euler equations.

(2)

The estimate (
1.42
) is a key observation to prove the continuous dependence of solutions, as well as Theorem
1.3
.

1.5.
Strategy of the proof

We provide a strategy of proofs
for Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
.

1.5.1.
Outline proof of Theorem
1.2

The main idea is as follows.

\bullet
Step 1: Energy estimates
. For
\mathbf{u}
and
h
, we use the hyperbolic system to get its energy(see Theorem
3.1
)

\|(h,\mathbf{u})\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\lesssim\|(h_{0},\mathbf{u}_{0})\|_{H^{s}}\exp\left(\int^{t}_{0}\|dh,d\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\right).

To bound
\|\mathbf{w}\|_{H^{s_{0}}_{x}}
, we only need to consider
\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}
and
\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}_{x}}
. Recalling the second equation in System (
1.20
), by using classical method, we therefore get

\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\exp\left(\int^{t}_{0}\|dh,d\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\right).

However, the bound of
\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}_{x}}
is much difficult, for there are two terms
\nabla^{3}\mathbf{u}
and
\nabla^{3}h
if we operate spatial second-order derivatives on the third equation in (
1.20
). Fortunately, we find a good decomposition for
\partial^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\mathbf{u}
(see Lemma
2.16
for details):

\partial^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}\approx\mathrm{vort}^{\alpha}\mathbf{w}+\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma})-u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}(u^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha}-u^{\alpha}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma})+(d\mathbf{u},dh)\cdot(du,dh),

whose role is very similar to Hodge decomposition in non-relativistic fluid dynamics. This allows us to derive a good transport equation for
\mathbf{G}
(see Definition (
1.18
)):

u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}(G^{\alpha}-F^{\alpha})=\partial^{\alpha}\Gamma+E^{\alpha}.

(1.49)

Above,

\begin{split}&\Gamma\approx d\mathbf{u}\cdot d\mathbf{w},\\
&\mathbf{F}\approx(d\mathbf{u},dh)\cdot d\mathbf{w}+(d\mathbf{u},dh)\cdot(d\mathbf{u},dh)\cdot\mathbf{w},\\
&\mathbf{E}\approx(d\mathbf{u},dh)\cdot d^{2}\mathbf{w}+(d\mathbf{u},dh)\cdot(d\mathbf{u},dh)\cdot(d\mathbf{u},dh)\cdot\mathbf{w}\\
&\quad\quad+(d\mathbf{u},dh)\cdot\mathbf{w}\cdot(d^{2}\mathbf{u},d^{2}h)+(d\mathbf{u},dh)\cdot(d\mathbf{u},dh)\cdot d\mathbf{w}.\end{split}

See Lemma
2.17
for details. So we transfer our goal to bound
\|\mathbf{G}\|_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}-2}_{x}}
. Since (
1.49
), we can see

\frac{d}{dt}\|\Lambda^{s_{0}-2}_{x}(\mathbf{G}-\mathbf{F})\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}=\textrm{H}_{1}+\textrm{good \ terms},

(1.50)

where
\textrm{H}_{1}
is the most difficult term

\textrm{H}_{1}=\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\partial^{\alpha}\left\{\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\big((u^{0})^{-1}\Gamma\big)\right\}\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\left(G_{\alpha}-F_{\alpha}\right)dxd\tau,

(1.51)

and ”good terms” can be estimated by product and commutator estimates. To calculate
\textrm{H}_{1}
, we divide (
1.51
) into two parts

\textrm{H}_{1}=\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\partial_{\alpha}(\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\big((u^{0})^{-1}\Gamma\big))\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}G^{\alpha}dx}_{\equiv\mathrm{H}_{11}}\underbrace{-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\partial_{\alpha}(\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\big((u^{0})^{-1}\Gamma\big))\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}F^{\alpha}dx}_{\equiv\mathrm{H}_{12}}.

For
\mathrm{H}_{11}
, integrating it parts, which yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{11}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\partial_{\alpha}(\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\big((u^{0})^{-1}\Gamma\big)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}G^{\alpha})dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\big((u^{0})^{-1}\Gamma\big)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}(\partial_{\alpha}G^{\alpha})dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\big((u^{0})^{-1}\Gamma\big)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}G^{0}dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\big((u^{0})^{-1}\Gamma\big)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}(\partial_{\alpha}G^{\alpha})dx.

(1.52)

For the first term in (
1.52
), it’s a lower order term compared with the left-hand side of (
1.50
). So we can put it into the left-hand side of (
1.50
). For the second term in (
1.52
), we can bound it by product and commutator estimates.

Similarly, we can calculate
\mathrm{H}_{12}
by

\begin{split}\mathrm{H}_{12}=&-\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\big((u^{0})^{-1}\Gamma\big)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}F^{0}dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\big((u^{0})^{-1}\Gamma\big)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}(\partial_{\alpha}F^{\alpha})dx.\end{split}

(1.53)

For the first term in (
1.53
), it’s also a lower order term compared with the left-hand side of (
1.50
). So we can also put it into the left-hand side of (
1.50
). For the second term in (
1.53
), a delicate analysis can lead us to get the desired bounds. Summing up the above outcome, and using Young inequality, Gronwall inequality, we can obtain the following desired energy estimate

E_{s}(t)\leq E_{0}\exp\left(5M(t)\cdot\mathrm{e}^{5M(t)}\right),

(1.54)

where

\begin{split}&E_{0}=C\left(\|(h_{0},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}_{0})\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s_{0}}}+\|(h_{0},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}_{0})\|^{10}_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\|^{10}_{H^{s_{0}}}\right),\\
&M(t)={\int^{t}_{0}}(\|d\mathbf{u},dh\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|d\mathbf{u},dh\|_{\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}})d\tau.\end{split}

See Section
3.1
for details.

Therefore, the bound of total energy
E_{s}(t)
depends on the Strichartz estimate for

{\int^{t}_{0}}(\|d\mathbf{u},dh\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|d\mathbf{u},dh\|_{\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}})d\tau.

(1.55)

\bullet
Step 2: Reduction to a existence result with small, smooth, and supported initial data
. For general large data, it’s hard to prove (
1.55
). For small data problem, we always have some idea to start. Since the system (
1.20
) has finite propagation speed, by a standard compactness method, scaling and localization technique, we can reduce Theorem
1.2
to the following statement. For any smooth, supported data
(h_{0},\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
satisfying

\begin{split}&\|h_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{u}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\|_{H^{s_{0}}}\leq\epsilon_{3},\end{split}

then the Cauchy problem (
1.20
) admits a smooth solution
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})
on
[-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which has the following properties:

\mathrm{(i)}
energy estimates

\begin{split}&\|h\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-1,1]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-1,1]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-1,1]}H_{x}^{s_{0}}}\leq\epsilon_{2}.\end{split}

(1.56)

\mathrm{(ii)}
dispersive estimate for
h
and
\mathbf{u}

\|dh,d\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}_{[-1,1]}C^{\delta}_{x}}+\|dh,\nabla\mathbf{u}_{+},d\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}_{[-1,1]}\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}}\leq\epsilon_{2},

(1.57)

\mathrm{(iii)}
dispersive estimate for the linear equation

Let
f
satisfy
the equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{g}f=0,\qquad(t,x)\in[-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&f(t_{0},\cdot)=f_{0}\in H_{x}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\partial_{t}f(t_{0},\cdot)=f_{1}\in H_{x}^{r-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}).\end{cases}

(1.58)

For each
1\leq r\leq s+1
, the Cauchy problem (
1.58
) is well-posed in
H_{x}^{r}\times H_{x}^{r-1}
, and the following estimate holds:

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{k}f\|_{L^{2}_{[-1,1]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}},\quad k<r-1.

(1.59)

See Section
4.1
and Section
4.2
for the complete proof.

\bullet
Step 3: Reduction to a bootstrap argument
. When the problem is reduced to small smooth supported initial data
(h_{0},\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
, then we can consider it as a perturbation problem around Minkowski space. Following
[
50
]
, we further reduce the proof of (
1.56
)-(
1.59
) to: there is a continuous functional
\Re:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
, satisfying
\Re(0,\mathbf{e},\mathbf{0})=0
(for
\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{e}
, see (
4.64
) below), so that for each
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\in\mathcal{H}
satisfying
\Re(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
the following hold:

\mathrm{(i)}
the function
h,\mathbf{u}
, and
\mathbf{w}
satisfies
\Re(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\leq\epsilon_{1}
;

\mathrm{(ii)}
the estimates (
1.56
)-(
1.57
) and (
1.59
) both hold.

By using energy estimates in Theorem
3.2
, the estimate (
1.56
) follows from (
1.57
). Hence, the proof of
\Re(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\leq\epsilon_{1}
and (
1.57
)-(
1.59
) are the core. See Section
4.3
for details.

\bullet
Step 4: Definition of
\Re
and regularity of characteristic null surface
.

Following
[
50
]
, for
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
,
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and let
\Sigma_{\theta,r}
be the flowout of the set
\theta\cdot\mathbf{x}=r-2
along the null geodesic flow with respect of
\mathbf{g}
in the direction
\theta
at
t=-2
. Here
\mathbf{g}
is a localization of
g
(see in (
4.65
)). Let
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\theta}
be given orthonormal coordinates on the hyperplane in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
perpendicular to
\theta
. Then,
\Sigma_{\theta,r}
is of the form

\Sigma_{\theta,r}=\left\{(t,\mathbf{x}):\theta\cdot\mathbf{x}-\phi_{\theta,r}=0\right\}

for a smooth function
\phi_{\theta,r}(t,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\theta})
.
For a given
\theta
, the family
\{\Sigma_{\theta,r},\ r\in\mathbb{R}\}
defines a foliation of
[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, by characteristic hypersurfaces with respect to
\mathbf{g}
.

We define (see (
5.4
) for details)

\Re=\sup_{\theta,r}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|d\phi_{\theta,r}-dt|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0},2,{\Sigma_{\theta,r}}}.

(1.60)

For simplicity, let us suppose
\theta=(0,0,1)
and
r=0
. In this case, we set
\Sigma=\Sigma_{\theta,r}
and
\phi=\phi_{\theta,r}
. To prove
\Re\leq\epsilon_{1}
and catch the geometry properties of
\Sigma
, let us introduce a null frame along
\Sigma
as follows. Let

V=(dr)^{*},

where
r
is the foliation of
\Sigma
, and where
*
denotes the identification of covectors and vectors induced by
\mathbf{g}
. Define a null frame with respect to
\mathbf{g}
by setting

l=\left<dt,dx_{3}-d\phi\right>^{-1}_{\mathbf{g}}\left(dx_{3}-d\phi\right)^{*},\quad\underline{l}=l+2\partial_{t},

and choosing orthonormalized vector fields tangent to the fixed-time slice
\Sigma^{t}
of
\Sigma
.

Based on this null frame, the bound of
\Re
relies on the connection coefficients on
\Sigma
. Based on the well-known Raychaudhuri equation
8
8

8

It is introduced by Christodoulou-Klainerman
[
14
]
and Klainerman
[
30
]
.
, together with the bound of
|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|(h,\mathbf{u})|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0},2,{\Sigma}}
and
|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{W}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0}-1,2,{\Sigma}}
(see Corollary
5.4
and Lemma
5.8
), then
\Re\lesssim\epsilon_{2}
is established in Proposition
5.9
. See Section
5.3
for details.

\bullet
Step 5: Strichartz estimates
. In this step, our goal is to derive the Strichartz estimate (
1.57
). A classical idea is to use the Strichartz estimate of linear wave and Duhamel’s principle. Only using System (
1.20
), the classical Duhamel’s principle, and (
1.58
)-(
1.59
), are not sufficient for us to prove (
1.57
). This pushes us to introduce a modified Duhamel’s principle (see Lemma
2.13
below). By Lemma
2.13
, we are able to establish the following Strichartz estimate for a non-homogeneous linear wave equation. For any
1\leq r\leq s+1
, for each
t_{0}\in[-2,2)
, then the Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}f=\mathbf{g}^{0\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}F,\qquad(t,x)\in(t_{0},2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&(f,\partial_{t}f)|_{t=t_{0}}=(f_{0},F(t_{0},\cdot)),\end{cases}

(1.61)

admits a solution
f\in C([-2,2],H_{x}^{r})\times C^{1}([-2,2],H_{x}^{r-1})
and the following estimates holds, provided
k<r-1
,

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{k}f,\left<\nabla\right>^{k-1}df\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r}}.

(1.62)

Seeing (
1.20
) again,
h
satisfies a good wave equation, while
\mathbf{u}
does not. So we find a good component
9
9

9

This is inspired by Wang’s work
[
56
]
on 3D non-relativistic compressible Euler equations.

\mathbf{u}_{+}
instead of
\mathbf{u}
. Precisely, the good wave equation for
h
and
\mathbf{u}_{+}
take the form (see Lemma
2.18
below):

\begin{cases}&{\square}_{g}h\approx(d\mathbf{u},dh)\cdot(d\mathbf{u},dh),\\
&{\square}_{g}\mathbf{u}_{+}\approx g^{0\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{u}_{-})+\nabla(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{u}_{-})+(d\mathbf{u},dh)\cdot(d\mathbf{u},dh)+\textrm{lower\ order\ terms}.\end{cases}

(1.63)

Since (
1.63
),
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{u}_{-}
is the possible trouble term. Fortunately, using the elliptic setting for
\mathbf{u}_{-}
(see (
1.26
)) and the transport structure for
\mathbf{W}
(see Lemma
2.15
), then a new space-time elliptic estimate for
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{u}_{-}
(see Lemma
2.19
below) holds. This combing with (
1.61
), (
1.62
), and (
1.63
), we therefore obtain the desired Strichartz estimate

\|dh,d\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}C^{\delta}_{x}}+\|dh,d\mathbf{u}_{+},d\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}_{[-1,1]}\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}}\leq\epsilon_{2}.

For the entire proof, we refer the readers to Section
5.4
.

\bullet
Step 6: The proof of (
1.61
)-(
1.62
)
. Through the above steps, we have obtained sufficient regularity of the characteristic hypersurfaces to control the characteristic hypersurfaces. This allows us to use the idea of constructing approximate solutions by wave-packets
[
50
,
47
]
. The whole proof is presented in Section
6
.

\bullet
Step 7: Continuous dependence.
In the proof of continuous dependence, for quasilinear system (
1.20
) with rough data, there is loss of derivatives if we only use the Strichartz estimates of solutions. To overcome this difficulty, using frequency envelope approach, together with the key Strichartz estimate (
1.41
)-(
1.42
), we can prove the continuous dependence. We refer Section
7
for details.

1.5.2.
Outline proof of Theorem
1.3

The main idea is as follows.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 1: Energy estimates}
. By delicate calculations, we find that the bound of
\|h\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{2}}
is controlled by the quantity

\tilde{E}_{0}\exp\left\{5{\int^{t}_{0}}\|(dh,d\mathbf{u})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau\cdot\exp\big(5{\int^{t}_{0}}\|(dh,d\mathbf{u})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau\big)\right\},

where
\tilde{E}_{0}
is determined by the initial norm
\|h_{0}\|_{H^{s}}
,
\|{\mathbf{u}}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}
, and
\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\|_{H^{2}}
. We refer the reader to Section
3.2
.

So the main goal is to control the following Strichartz estimate

\int^{t}_{0}\|dh,d\mathbf{u}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}d\tau.

(1.64)

\bullet\ \textit{Step 2: Constructing a strong solution as a limit of smooth solutions}
.
Since the initial data is not so smooth, we apply a mollifier to
(h_{0},\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
. Let
P_{j}=\sum_{j^{\prime}\leq j}P_{j^{\prime}}
and
P_{j^{\prime}}
is a LP operator with frequency supports
\{2^{j^{\prime}-3}\leq|\xi|\leq 2^{j^{\prime}+3}\}
. We introduce the smooth sequence
(h_{0j},\mathbf{u}_{0j})
by

\begin{split}&\mathring{\mathbf{u}}_{0j}=P_{\leq j}\mathring{\mathbf{u}}_{0},\quad u^{0}_{0j}=\sqrt{1+|\mathring{\mathbf{u}}_{0j}|^{2}},\\
&h_{0j}=P_{\leq j}h_{0},\quad\mathbf{u}_{0j}=(u^{0}_{0j},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}_{0j}),\end{split}

Considering (
1.15
), so we define

\mathbf{w}_{0j}=\mathrm{vort}(\mathrm{e}^{h_{0j}}\mathbf{u}_{0j}).

Therefore,
\left\{(h_{0j},\mathbf{u}_{0j},\mathbf{w}_{0j})\right\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a smooth initial sequence.

Considering above initial datum
(h_{0j},\mathbf{u}_{0j},\mathbf{w}_{0j})
, there exists positive numbers
T^{*}
,
{M}_{2}
, and
M_{3}
(
T^{*}
,
{M}_{2}
and
{M}_{3}
only depends on
C_{0},c_{0},s
and
M_{*}
) such that a sequence solutions of (
1.20
) satisfy
(h_{j},\mathbf{u}_{j})\in C([0,T^{*}];H_{x}^{s})\cap C^{1}([0,T^{*}];H_{x}^{s-1})
,
\mathbf{w}_{j}\in C([0,T^{*}];H_{x}^{2})\cap C^{1}([0,T^{*}];H_{x}^{1})
. Moreover, for all
j\geq 1
,
h_{j}
and
\mathbf{u}_{j}
satisfy a uniform Strichartz estimate

\|dh_{j},d\mathbf{u}_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}]}L_{x}^{\infty}}\leq{M}_{2}.

(1.65)

Additionally, for
\frac{s}{2}\leq r\leq 3
, considering

\begin{cases}\square_{{g}_{j}}f_{j}=0,\qquad[0,T^{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
(f_{j},\partial_{t}f_{j})|_{t=0}=(f_{0j},f_{1j}),\end{cases}

(1.66)

where
(f_{0j},f_{1j})=(P_{\leq j}f_{0},P_{\leq j}f_{1})
and
(f_{0},f_{1})\in H_{x}^{r}\times H^{r-1}_{x}
, there is a unique solution
f_{j}
on
[0,T^{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that for
a\leq r-\frac{s}{2}
,

\begin{split}&\|\left<\nabla\right>^{a-1}d{f}_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq M_{3}(\|{f}_{0}\|_{{H}_{x}^{r}}+\|{f}_{1}\|_{{H}_{x}^{r-1}}),\\
&\|{f}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H^{r}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}{f}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H^{r-1}_{x}}\leq M_{3}(\|{f}_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|{f}_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}).\end{split}

(1.67)

Based on (
1.65
)-(
1.67
), we are able to obtain a strong solution of Theorem
1.3
as a limit of the sequence
(h_{j},\mathbf{u}_{j},\mathbf{w}_{j})
. Please refer Section
8.1
for details.

It remains for us to prove (
1.65
)-(
1.67
).

\bullet\ \textit{Step 3: Obtaining a sequence of solutions on small time-intervals}
. Note
\|{\mathbf{w}}_{0j}\|_{H^{2+r}}\lesssim 2^{jr}
(
r\geq 0
). By using compactness, scaling, and physical localization, we shall get a sequence of small initial datum. By using Proposition
8.2
, we can prove the existence of small solutions with these small datum. On one hand, Proposition
8.2
can be derived from (
1.56
)-(
1.57
). See Section
8.2
for details.

On the other hand, returning back from small solutions to large solutions
(h_{j},\mathbf{u}_{j},\mathbf{w}_{j})
, then the time of existence of solutions
(h_{j},\mathbf{u}_{j},\mathbf{w}_{j})
depends on
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. Because the norm
\|{\mathbf{w}}_{0j}\|_{H^{2+}}
depending on
j
. Moreover, we precisly calculate
T^{*}_{j}=2^{-\delta_{1}j}[E(0)]^{-1}
(
\delta_{1}=\frac{s-2}{40}
and
E(0)=C(M_{*}+M_{*}^{5})
, c.f (
8.4
) and (
8.70
)). On
[0,T^{*}_{j}]
, the solutions
h_{j}
and
\mathbf{u}_{j}
yields a higher-order Strichartz estimates,

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{a-1}dh_{j},\left<\nabla\right>^{a-1}d\mathbf{u}_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}_{j}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C(1+E(0)),\quad a<s-1.

(1.68)

Therefore, by choosing a suitable number
a=9\delta_{1}
, for high frequency
k\geq j
, then we get

\|P_{k}dh_{j},P_{k}d\mathbf{u}_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}_{j}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C(1+E^{3}(0))2^{-\delta_{1}k}2^{-7\delta_{1}j}.

(1.69)

Decompose

\begin{split}d\mathbf{u}_{j}=P_{\geq j}d\mathbf{u}_{j}+\textstyle{\sum}^{j-1}_{k=1}\textstyle{\sum}_{m=k}^{j-1}P_{k}(d\mathbf{u}_{m+1}-d\mathbf{u}_{m})+\textstyle{\sum}^{j-1}_{k=1}P_{k}d\mathbf{u}_{k}.\end{split}

Then we need to see if there is a similar estimates for
P_{k}(d\mathbf{u}_{m+1}-d\mathbf{u}_{m})
. By using a Strichartz estimates for linear wave, for
k<j
, we shall also obtain

\|P_{k}(dh_{m+1}-dh_{m}),P_{k}(d\mathbf{u}_{m+1}-d\mathbf{u}_{m})\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}_{m+1}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C(1+E^{3}(0))2^{-\delta_{1}k}2^{-6\delta_{1}m}.

(1.70)

We prove (
1.69
)-(
1.70
) in Section
8.3.2
.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 4: Extending a sequence of solutions to a regular time-interval}
. Compared (
1.68
) with (
1.69
)-(
1.70
), there is a loss of derivatives in (
1.69
)-(
1.70
). The benifit relies on some decay estimates (
1.69
)-(
1.70
) on frequency number
j
and
k
. Inspired by Bahouri-Chemin
[
11
]
, Tataru
[
51
]
and Ai-Ifrim-Tataru
[
2
]
, if we can always extend it with a length
\approx T_{j}^{*}
of time intervals, and with a number
\approx(T_{j}^{*})^{-1}
, then we shall sum these estimates up and obtain a uniform Strichartz estimates
\|dh_{j},d\mathbf{u}_{j}\|_{L^{1}_{[0,T^{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}
and
\|dh_{j},d\mathbf{u}_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}
on a regular time-interval
[0,T^{*}]
. In fact, we can extend it in this way from
[0,T^{*}_{j}]
to
[0,T^{*}_{N_{0}}]
. We first extend it from
[0,T^{*}_{j}]
to
[0,T^{*}_{j-1}]
. In this process, the growth in Strichartz and energy estimates can be calculated precisely. Then we conclude it by induction method. Finally, we get

T^{*}=2^{-\delta_{1}N_{0}}[E(0)]^{-1},

where
N_{0}
is an fixed integer depending on
c_{0},s,C_{0}
and
M_{*}
(see (
8.73
) below). Furthermore,

\|h_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{u}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H_{x}^{2}},

and

\|dh_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|d\mathbf{u}_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}},

are uniformly bounded (See (
8.176
)-(
8.177
)). Please refer Subsection
8.3.3
for details.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 5: Strichartz estimates for linear wave on the regular time interval}
.
When the solutions in Step 4 are extended, then the corresponding solutions of linear wave are also extended on the same time-interval. By using a similar idea, summing up these bounds, we can prove (
1.66
)-(
1.67
). We refer the readers to Section
8.3.4
for the whole proof.

1.6.
Organization of the rest paper

In the rest of the paper, Section
2
mainly introduce some basic key lemmas, including commutator and product estimates; modified Duhamel’s principle; new transport equations and some elliptic estimates. Next, in Section
3
, we mainly prove two key energy theorems, i.e. Theorem
3.2
and Theorem
3.3
. In Section
4
, we give a precise proof of the existence and uniqueness of solutions for Theorem
1.2
. Then we give a precise proof for Proposition
4.3
in Section
5
. In Section
6
, we give a proof for Proposition
5.13
. The next Section
7
give a proof of continuous dependence of solutions in Theorem
1.2
. Additionally, Section
8
presents the proof of the well-posedness of solutions in Theorem
1.3
. Finally, we prove some auxiliary lemmas and give a guidance of notations in Section
9
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Commutator estimates and product estimates

We start by recalling some commutator and product estimates, which have been proved in references
[
29
,
50
,
54
,
42
,
8
]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

[
29
]

Let
a\geq 0
. Then for any scalar function
f_{1},f_{2}
, we have

\|\Lambda_{x}^{a}(f_{1}f_{2})-(\Lambda_{x}^{a}f_{1})f_{2}\|_{L_{x}^{2}}\lesssim\|\Lambda_{x}^{a-1}f_{1}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\|\nabla f_{2}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}+\|f_{1}\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\|\Lambda_{x}^{a}f_{2}\|_{L_{x}^{q}},

where
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{2}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

[
29
]

Let
a\geq 0
. For any scalar function
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, we have

\|f_{1}f_{2}\|_{H_{x}^{a}}\lesssim\|f_{1}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}\|f_{2}\|_{H_{x}^{a}}+\|f_{2}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{a}}.

Lemma 2.3
.

[
8
]

Let
F(u)
be a smooth function of
u
,
F(0)=0
and
u\in L^{\infty}_{x}
. For any
a\geq 0
, we have

\|F(u)\|_{H_{x}^{a}}\lesssim\|u\|_{H_{x}^{a}}(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}).

Lemma 2.4
.

[
50
]

Suppose that
0\leq r,r^{\prime}<\frac{3}{2}
and
r+r^{\prime}>\frac{3}{2}
. Then for any scalar function
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, the following estimate holds:

\|f_{1}f_{2}\|_{H^{r+r^{\prime}-\frac{3}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C_{r,r^{\prime}}\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\|f_{2}\|_{H^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

If
-r\leq r^{\prime}\leq r
and
r>\frac{3}{2}
then

\|f_{1}f_{2}\|_{H^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C_{r,r^{\prime}}\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\|f_{2}\|_{H^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Lemma 2.5
.

[
54
]

Let
0\leq a<1
. Then

\|\Lambda_{x}^{a}(f_{1}f_{2})\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\lesssim\|f_{1}\|_{\dot{B}^{a}_{\infty,2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\|f_{2}\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|f_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\|f_{2}\|_{\dot{H}^{a}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Lemma 2.6
.

[
54
]

Let
0<a<1
. Then

\|\Lambda_{x}^{a}(f_{1}f_{2}f_{3})\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\lesssim\|f_{i}\|_{H^{1+a}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\textstyle{\prod_{j\neq i}}\|f_{j}\|_{H^{1}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Lemma 2.7
.

[
8
]

Let
0<a<1
. Let
b>a
and sufficiently close to
a
. Then

\|f_{1}f_{2}\|_{\dot{B}^{a}_{\infty,2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\lesssim\|f_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|f_{1}\|_{\dot{B}^{a}_{\infty,2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|f_{2}\|_{C^{b}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\|f_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}.

(2.1)

Lemma 2.8
.

[
8
]

Let
0\leq a<1
and
\mathbf{u}=(u^{0},u^{1},u^{2},u^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
. Denote the Riesz operator
\mathbf{R}=\nabla^{2}(-\Delta)^{-1}
. Then

\|[\mathbf{R},(u^{0})^{-1}u^{i}\partial_{i}]f\|_{\dot{H}^{a}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\lesssim\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\dot{B}^{1+a}_{\infty,\infty}}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\dot{B}^{1}_{\infty,\infty}}\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{a}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Lemma 2.9
.

[
8
]

Let
2<s_{1}\leq s_{2}
. Let
\mathbf{T}
be defined in (
1.32
). Then

\{2^{(s_{1}-1)j}\|[P_{j},\mathbf{T}]f\|_{\dot{H}^{s_{2}-s_{1}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\}_{l^{2}_{j}}\lesssim\|\nabla\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{s_{2}-1}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{H^{s_{2}}}\|f\|_{{H}^{1}}.

Lemma 2.10
.

[
8
]

Let
0<a<1
and
\mathbf{u}=(u^{0},u^{1},u^{2},u^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
. Then

\|[\Lambda^{a}_{x},(u^{0})^{-1}u^{i}\partial_{i}]f\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\lesssim\|\nabla\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{a}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Lemma 2.11
.

[
8
]

Let
2<s_{1}\leq s_{2}
. Let
\mathbf{g}
be a Lorentz metric and
\mathbf{g}^{00}=-1
. We have

\begin{split}\left\{2^{(s_{1}-1)j}\|[\square_{\mathbf{g}},P_{j}]f\|_{\dot{H}_{x}^{s_{2}-s_{1}}}\right\}_{l^{2}_{j}}\lesssim&\ \|df\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{\dot{H}_{x}^{s_{2}-1}}+\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}\|df\|_{\dot{H}_{x}^{s_{2}-1}}.\end{split}

(2.2)

We next introduce a identity, which is very useful in our paper.

Lemma 2.12

(
[
19
]
, Lemma 4.1)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}=(A^{0},A^{1},A^{2},A^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
be a one-form. Let
\mathrm{vort}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{A})
be defined in (
1.14
). Then the following equality

\partial_{\alpha}A_{\beta}-\partial_{\beta}A_{\alpha}=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u^{\gamma}\mathrm{vort}^{\delta}(\mathbf{A})+u_{\alpha}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\beta}A_{\kappa}-u_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\alpha}A_{\kappa}+u_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}A_{\alpha}-u_{\alpha}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}A_{\beta},

holds.

Remark 2.1
.

Let
(h,\mathbf{u})
be a solution of (
1.13
). Let
\mathbf{w}
be defined in (
1.15
). By Lemma
2.12
, we can get

\partial_{\alpha}u_{\beta}-\partial_{\beta}u_{\alpha}=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\mathrm{e}^{-h}u^{\gamma}w^{\delta}-u_{\beta}\partial_{\alpha}h+u_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}h,

(2.3)

and

\partial_{\alpha}w_{\beta}-\partial_{\beta}w_{\alpha}=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u^{\gamma}\mathrm{vort}^{\delta}(\mathbf{w})-u_{\alpha}u^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}w_{\beta}+u_{\alpha}u^{\gamma}\partial_{\beta}w_{\gamma}+u_{\beta}u^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}w_{\alpha}-u_{\beta}u^{\gamma}\partial_{\alpha}w_{\gamma}.

(2.4)

Additionally, we have

w^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\alpha}=w^{\kappa}\partial_{\alpha}u_{\kappa}-u_{\alpha}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h,

(2.5)

and

\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\delta}=\mathrm{e}^{-h}w^{\alpha}u^{\beta}-\mathrm{e}^{-h}u^{\alpha}w^{\beta}-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\delta}\partial_{\gamma}h.

(2.6)

In the following, we transfer to introduce a modified Duhamel’s principle for acoustic metric.

2.2.
Modified Duhamel’s principle

Lemma 2.13
.

Let the metric
g
is defined in (
1.16
). If
f
is the solution of

\begin{cases}\square_{g}f=0,\qquad t>\tau,\\
(f,\partial_{t}f)|_{t=\tau}=(F(\tau,x),0),\end{cases}

then

\phi(t,x)=\int^{t}_{0}f(t,x;\tau)d\tau,

solves the linear wave equation

\begin{cases}\square_{g}\phi=g^{0\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}F,\\
(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)|_{t=0}=(0,F(0,x)).\end{cases}

(2.7)

Proof.

We first note
\square_{g}=g^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}
and
g^{00}=-1
. By direct calculations, we get

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\phi&=\int^{t}_{0}\partial_{t}f(t,x;\tau)d\tau+f(t,x;t)\\
&=\int^{t}_{0}\partial_{t}f(t,x;\tau)d\tau+F(t,x).\end{split}

(2.8)

Taking the operator
\partial_{t}
on (
2.8
), we have

\begin{split}-\partial^{2}_{t}\phi&=-\int^{t}_{0}\partial^{2}_{t}f(t,x;\tau)d\tau-\partial_{t}f(t,x;\tau)|_{\tau=t}-\partial_{t}F\\
&=-\int^{t}_{0}\partial^{2}_{t}f(t,x;\tau)d\tau-\partial_{t}F.\end{split}

(2.9)

Taking the operator
\partial_{i}
on (
2.9
), it follows

\begin{split}\partial_{i}\partial_{t}\phi&=\int^{t}_{0}\partial_{i}\partial_{t}f(t,x;\tau)d\tau+\partial_{i}F.\end{split}

(2.10)

On the other hand, we also have

\partial_{ij}\phi=\int^{t}_{0}\partial_{ij}f(t,x;\tau)d\tau,

(2.11)

Combining (
2.9
), (
2.10
), and (
2.11
), we can derive that

\begin{split}\square_{g}\phi(t,x)&=\int^{t}_{0}\square_{g}f(t,x;\tau)d\tau-g^{0i}\partial_{i}F(t,x)+\partial_{t}F(t,x)\\
&=g^{0\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}F.\end{split}

Furthermore, we can verify the initial data as

(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)|_{t=0}=(0,F(0,x)).

At this stage, we complete the proof of Lemma
2.13
.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

Due to the acoustical metric, so we derive this modified Duhamel’s principle.

2.3.
Hyperbolic formulations and new transport system

)-(

), which will be used to derive energy estimates of the velocity and enthalpy.

Lemma 2.14

(
[

]
,Theorem 13.1)
.

Let

be a solution of (

)-(

A^{\alpha}(\mathbf{U})\partial_{\alpha}\mathbf{U}=0,

(2.12)

where

A^{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}(\varrho+p)^{-2}\varrho^{\prime}(p)u^{0}&-(\varrho+p)^{-1}\frac{u^{1}}{u_{0}}&-(\varrho+p)^{-1}\frac{u^{2}}{u_{0}}&-(\varrho+p)^{-1}\frac{u^{3}}{u_{0}}\\
-(\varrho+p)^{-1}\frac{u^{1}}{u_{0}}&u^{0}(1+\frac{u^{1}u_{1}}{u^{0}u_{0}})&u^{0}\frac{u^{1}u_{2}}{u^{0}u_{0}}&u^{0}\frac{u^{1}u_{3}}{u^{0}u_{0}}\\

(2.13)

A^{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}(\varrho+p)^{-2}\varrho^{\prime}(p)u^{1}&-(\varrho+p)^{-1}&0&0\\

(2.16)

(2.17)

(2.18)

We next introduce a decomposition for
\partial_{\gamma}\partial^{\gamma}\mathbf{u}
, whose role is very similar to Hodge’s decomposition in non-relativistic fluids.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
(h,\mathbf{u})
be a solution of (
1.13
). Then we have the following formula

\begin{split}\partial_{\gamma}(\partial^{\gamma}u^{\alpha})=&\mathrm{vort}^{\alpha}(\mathrm{vort}\mathbf{u})+\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma})-u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}(u^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha}-u^{\alpha}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma})\\
&+2u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}u^{\gamma}-2u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma}\partial^{\beta}u^{\alpha}-u^{\alpha}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\beta}\partial^{\gamma}u^{\beta}+u^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}u^{\beta}.\end{split}

(2.20)

Proof.

Firstly, we have
\mathrm{vort}^{\alpha}(\mathrm{vort}\mathbf{u})=\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta\eta\mu\nu}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}\left(u^{\eta}\partial^{\mu}u^{\nu}\right)
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{vort}^{\alpha}(\mathrm{vort}\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-\delta^{\alpha}_{\eta}\delta^{\gamma}_{\mu}\delta^{\beta}_{\nu}+\delta^{\alpha}_{\eta}\delta^{\beta}_{\mu}\delta^{\gamma}_{\nu}-\delta^{\beta}_{\eta}\delta^{\alpha}_{\mu}\delta^{\gamma}_{\nu}+\delta^{\beta}_{\eta}\delta^{\gamma}_{\mu}\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu}-\delta^{\gamma}_{\eta}\delta^{\beta}_{\mu}\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu}+\delta^{\gamma}_{\eta}\delta^{\alpha}_{\mu}\delta^{\beta}_{\nu})u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}\left(u^{\eta}\partial^{\mu}u^{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha}\partial^{\gamma}u^{\beta}-u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}u^{\gamma}+u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}u^{\gamma}-u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\beta}\partial^{\gamma}u^{\alpha}

\displaystyle+u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma}\partial^{\beta}u^{\alpha}-u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}u^{\beta}+u_{\beta}u^{\alpha}\partial^{\gamma}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\beta})-u_{\beta}u^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma})

\displaystyle+u_{\beta}u^{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma})-u_{\beta}u^{\beta}\partial^{\gamma}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha})+u_{\beta}u^{\gamma}\partial^{\beta}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha})-u_{\beta}u^{\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\beta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}u^{\gamma}+u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma}\partial^{\beta}u^{\alpha}+u_{\beta}u^{\alpha}\partial^{\gamma}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\beta})-u_{\beta}u^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma})

\displaystyle+u_{\beta}u^{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma})-u_{\beta}u^{\beta}\partial^{\gamma}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha})+u_{\beta}u^{\gamma}\partial^{\beta}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha})-u_{\beta}u^{\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\beta}),

(2.21)

where we use the fact
u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\beta}=u^{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\gamma}(u^{\beta}u_{\beta})=0
. By a direct calculation for

\begin{split}u_{\beta}u^{\alpha}\partial^{\gamma}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\beta})=&u^{\alpha}\left\{\partial^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}(u_{\beta}u^{\beta})-(\partial^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\beta})u^{\beta}-2\partial_{\gamma}u_{\beta}\partial^{\gamma}u^{\beta}\right\}\\
=&-u_{\beta}u^{\alpha}\partial^{\gamma}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\beta})-2u^{\alpha}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\beta}\partial^{\gamma}u^{\beta},\end{split}

we then have

u_{\beta}u^{\alpha}\partial^{\gamma}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\beta})=-u^{\alpha}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\beta}\partial^{\gamma}u^{\beta}.

(2.22)

Thirdly, we can compute out

\displaystyle-u_{\beta}u^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma})=

\displaystyle-u^{\alpha}u_{\beta}\partial^{\beta}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}\left(-u^{\alpha}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma}\right)+u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\alpha}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}\left(-u^{\alpha}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma}\right)+u_{\kappa}\partial^{\kappa}u^{\alpha}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma}.

(2.23)

By using
u_{\beta}u^{\beta}=-1
, we get

\displaystyle u_{\beta}u^{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma})=

\displaystyle\partial^{\alpha}(-\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma}),

(2.24)

\displaystyle-u_{\beta}u^{\beta}\partial^{\gamma}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha})=

\displaystyle\partial^{\gamma}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha}).

(2.25)

Also, we can deduce

u_{\beta}u^{\gamma}\partial^{\beta}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha})=u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}(u^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha})-u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\alpha},

(2.26)

and

\displaystyle-u_{\beta}u^{\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{\gamma}u^{\beta})=

\displaystyle u^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}(-u_{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}u^{\beta})+u^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}u^{\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}(-u_{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}u^{\beta})+u^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}u^{\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}u^{\beta}.

(2.27)

Inserting (
2.22
), (
2.3
), (
2.24
), (
2.25
), (
2.26
), (
2.3
) to (
2.3
), we can obtain (
2.20
).
∎

Lemma 2.17

and
10

10

Calculating

\begin{split}E^{\alpha}=&\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}{W}_{\delta}-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}{W}_{\delta}+2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}hW_{\delta}\\

&+\mathrm{e}^{-h}w_{\kappa}{W}^{\kappa}\partial^{\alpha}h-\mathrm{e}^{-h}w_{\kappa}\partial^{\alpha}{W}^{\kappa}-\epsilon_{\kappa\ \gamma\delta}^{\ \alpha}\epsilon^{\delta\beta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-2}\mathrm{e}^{-h}u^{\gamma}u_{\beta}w_{\nu}{W}^{\kappa}\partial_{\mu}h\\

&+\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}{W}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\delta}+4\partial^{\gamma}u_{\kappa}\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\gamma}w^{\kappa}+2u^{\beta}\partial^{\gamma}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\beta}u^{\alpha}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\kappa}\\

&+(4u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\lambda}\partial^{\beta}u^{\gamma}-4u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma}\partial^{\beta}u_{\lambda}-2u_{\lambda}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\beta}\partial^{\gamma}u^{\beta}+2u^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\beta}\partial_{\lambda}u^{\beta})\partial^{\alpha}w^{\lambda}\\
&-2c_{s}^{-2}\partial_{\lambda}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h\partial^{\alpha}w^{\lambda}+6c_{s}^{-1}c^{\prime}_{s}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\lambda}h\partial^{\alpha}w^{\lambda}+2c_{s}^{-2}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\lambda}h\partial_{\kappa}(\partial^{\alpha}w^{\lambda})\\

&-2u^{\gamma}w^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}(\partial^{\alpha}u_{\kappa})+2u^{\gamma}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\lambda}u^{\lambda}\partial_{\gamma}(\partial^{\alpha}u_{\kappa})-2u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}w^{\beta}\\
&+2u_{\beta}u^{\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{\gamma}w^{\kappa})\partial^{\beta}u_{\kappa}-2u^{\kappa}u^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{\kappa}w^{\beta})+2u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}w^{\kappa}\partial^{\beta}u_{\kappa}\\
&-2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-3}c^{\prime}_{s}u_{\beta}u_{\eta}w_{\nu}\partial_{\gamma}h\partial_{\mu}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h+\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\eta}w_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h\\
&+\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u_{\eta}\partial_{\gamma}w_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h+\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u_{\eta}w_{\nu}\partial_{\kappa}h\partial_{\gamma}\partial_{\mu}u^{\kappa}\\

&+2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\mathrm{e}^{-h}u_{\beta}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}w_{\delta}\partial_{\kappa}h+2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\mathrm{e}^{-h}u_{\beta}w_{\delta}\partial_{\gamma}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h\\
&+2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\mathrm{e}^{-h}u_{\beta}w_{\delta}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}\partial_{\kappa}h-2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon^{\kappa\eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-3}c^{\prime}_{s}u_{\beta}u_{\eta}w_{\nu}\partial_{\gamma}h\partial_{\mu}h\partial_{\kappa}u_{\delta}\\
&+\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon^{\kappa\eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}w_{\nu}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\eta}\partial_{\mu}h\partial_{\kappa}u_{\delta}+\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon^{\kappa\eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\eta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}w_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}h\partial_{\kappa}u_{\delta}\\
&+\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon^{\kappa\eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u_{\eta}w_{\nu}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\delta}\partial_{\gamma}\partial_{\mu}h+\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon^{\kappa\eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u_{\eta}w_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}h\partial_{\gamma}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\delta}\\
&+2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-3}c^{\prime}_{s}u_{\beta}u_{\eta}w_{\nu}\partial_{\gamma}h\partial_{\mu}h\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa}-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\eta}w_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}h\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa}\\
&-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u_{\eta}\partial_{\gamma}w_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}h\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa}-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u_{\eta}w_{\nu}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}\partial_{\mu}h\\
&-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u_{\eta}w_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}h\partial_{\gamma}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa}+2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}c_{s}^{-3}c^{\prime}_{s}u_{\beta}u_{\eta}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}h\partial_{\nu}u_{\kappa}\partial_{\mu}h\\
&-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}(c^{-2}_{s}+2)u_{\beta}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}u_{\eta}\partial_{\nu}u_{\kappa}\partial_{\mu}h-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}(c^{-2}_{s}+2)u_{\eta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\nu}u_{\kappa}\partial_{\mu}h\\
&-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}(c^{-2}_{s}+2)u_{\beta}u_{\eta}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\mu}h\partial_{\gamma}\partial_{\nu}u_{\kappa}-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}(c^{-2}_{s}+2)u_{\beta}u_{\eta}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\nu}u_{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}\partial_{\mu}h,\end{split}

(2.31)

Proof.

Operating (
2.15
) with
-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}
, we can derive

\begin{split}&-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}(u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}{W}_{\delta})\\
=&\underbrace{-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}({W}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\delta})}_{\equiv\mathrm{R}_{1}}\underbrace{-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}(u_{\delta}{W}^{\kappa}u^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}u_{\kappa})}_{\equiv\mathrm{R}_{2}}+\underbrace{2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}\left(\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa}{W}_{\delta}\right)}_{\equiv\mathrm{R}_{0}}\\
&+\underbrace{2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}\big(u_{\eta}\partial_{\mu}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\nu}u_{\kappa}\big)}_{\equiv\mathrm{R}_{3}}+\underbrace{\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}(c_{s}^{-2}u_{\eta}w_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h)}_{\equiv\mathrm{R}_{4}}\\
&+\underbrace{2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}(\mathrm{e}^{-h}w_{\delta}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h)}_{\equiv\mathrm{R}_{5}}+\underbrace{\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon^{\kappa\eta\mu\nu}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}(c_{s}^{-2}u_{\eta}w_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}h\partial_{\kappa}u_{\delta})}_{\equiv\mathrm{R}_{6}}\\
&\underbrace{-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}(c_{s}^{-2}u_{\eta}w_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}h\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa})}_{\equiv\mathrm{R}_{7}}\underbrace{-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\epsilon_{\delta}^{\ \eta\mu\nu}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}\{(c^{-2}_{s}+2)u_{\eta}w^{\kappa}\partial_{\nu}u_{\kappa}\partial_{\mu}h\}}_{\equiv\mathrm{R}_{8}}.\end{split}

(2.32)

By the chain rule, the left side of (
2.32
) is

\displaystyle-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}(u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}{W}_{\delta})

\displaystyle=-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}{W}_{\delta}-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}(\partial_{\gamma}{W}_{\delta})

\displaystyle=-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}{W}_{\delta}-u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}(\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}{W}_{\delta})+\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}{W}_{\delta}

\displaystyle=u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}G^{\alpha}-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}{W}_{\delta}+\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}{W}_{\delta}.

(2.33)

From (
2.32
) and (
2.3
), we can see

\begin{split}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}G^{\alpha}&=\sum^{8}_{a=0}\mathrm{R}_{a}+\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}{W}_{\delta}-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}{W}_{\delta}.\end{split}

(2.34)

While, there are some difficult terms in
\mathrm{R}_{0},\mathrm{R}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{R}_{8}
. So we need to discuss them in a proper way. For
\mathrm{R}_{0}
, by the chain rule and (
1.13
), we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{R}_{0}=&-2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}\left(c^{-2}_{s}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h{W}_{\delta}\right)\\
=&4\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-3}c^{\prime}_{s}u_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}h\partial_{\kappa}hW_{\delta}-2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h\partial_{\gamma}W_{\delta}-2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}(\partial_{\gamma}h)W_{\delta}\\
=&-4\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-1}c^{\prime}_{s}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}h\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa}W_{\delta}-2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h\partial_{\gamma}W_{\delta}-2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}(\partial_{\gamma}h)W_{\delta}.\end{split}

(2.35)

Noting the last right term in (
2.35
), it can be written as

\begin{split}-2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}(\partial_{\gamma}h)W_{\delta}=&u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}(-2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}hW_{\delta})-4\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-3}c^{\prime}_{s}u^{\kappa}u_{\beta}\partial_{\kappa}h\partial_{\gamma}hW_{\delta}\\
&+2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}hW_{\delta}+2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}h\partial_{\kappa}W_{\delta}.\end{split}

(2.36)

Gathering (
2.35
) and (
2.36
), we then get

\begin{split}\mathrm{R}_{0}=&u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}(-2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}hW_{\delta})+2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}hW_{\delta}\\
&+2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}h\partial_{\kappa}W_{\delta}-2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}h\partial_{\gamma}W_{\delta}\\
=&u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}(-2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}hW_{\delta})+2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}hW_{\delta}\\
&+2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}c_{s}^{-2}u_{\beta}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}h\partial_{\kappa}W_{\delta}+2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\kappa}u^{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}W_{\delta}.\end{split}

(2.37)

For
\mathrm{R}_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}_{1}

\displaystyle=-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}({W}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\delta})

\displaystyle=-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}\partial_{\gamma}{W}^{\kappa}\partial_{\kappa}u_{\delta}-\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}u_{\beta}{W}^{\kappa}\partial_{\gamma}(\partial_{\kappa}u_{\delta})
