) and (
5.61
), so (
5.14
) holds. Thus, we finish the proof of Lemma
5.8
.
∎

5.3.
Regularity of the characteristic hypersurface

Our goal here is to establish the following:

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\in\mathcal{H}
so that
\Re(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
. Then

\Re(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\lesssim\epsilon_{2}.

(5.62)

Furthermore, for each
t\in[-2,2]
it holds that

\|d\phi_{\theta,r}(t,\cdot)-dt\|_{C^{1,\delta_{0}}_{x^{\prime}}}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}+\|d{\mathbf{g}}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

(5.63)

To prove Proposition
5.9
, let us first introduce a null frame on
\Sigma
.

5.3.1.
Null frame

Firstly, let

V=(dr)^{*},

where
r
is the defining function of the foliation
\Sigma
, and where
*
denotes the identification of covectors and vectors induced by
\mathbf{g}
. Then
V
is the null geodesic flow field tangent to
\Sigma
. Let

\sigma=dt(V),\qquad l=\sigma^{-1}V.

(5.64)

Thus
l
is the g-normal field to
\Sigma
normalized so that
dt(l)=1
, hence

l=\left<dt,dx_{3}-d\phi\right>^{-1}_{\mathbf{g}}\left(dx_{3}-d\phi\right)^{*}.

(5.65)

So the coefficients
l^{j}
are smooth functions of
h,\mathbf{u}
and
d\phi
. Conversely,

dx_{3}-d\phi=\left<l,\partial_{x_{3}}\right>^{-1}_{\mathbf{g}}l^{*}.

(5.66)

It also implies that
d\phi
is a smooth function of
h,\mathbf{u}
and the coefficients of
l
.

Next we introduce the vector fields
e_{a},a=1,2
tangent to the fixed-time slice
\Sigma^{t}
of
\Sigma
. We do this by applying Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization in the metric
\mathbf{g}
to the
\Sigma^{t}
-tangent vector fields
\partial_{x_{a}}+\partial_{x_{a}}\phi\partial_{x_{3}},a=1,2
.

Finally, we set

\underline{l}=l+2\partial_{t}.

Then
\{l,\underline{l},e_{1},e_{2}\}
is a null frame in the sense that

\displaystyle\left<l,\underline{l}\right>_{\mathbf{g}}=2,\qquad\qquad\ \ \ \ \ \ \left<e_{a},e_{b}\right>_{\mathbf{g}}=\delta_{ab},\ \ (a,b=1,2),

\displaystyle\left<l,l\right>_{\mathbf{g}}=\left<\underline{l},\underline{l}\right>_{\mathbf{g}}=0,\qquad\left<l,e_{a}\right>_{\mathbf{g}}=\left<\underline{l},e_{a}\right>_{\mathbf{g}}=0,\ \ (a=1,2).

The coefficients of each fields are smooth functions of
h
,
\mathbf{u}
and
d\phi
. Also, by
\Re(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
, we also have the pointwise bound

|e_{1}-\partial_{x_{1}}|+|e_{2}-\partial_{x_{2}}|+|l-(\partial_{t}+\partial_{x_{3}})|+|\underline{l}-(-\partial_{t}+\partial_{x_{3}})|\lesssim\epsilon_{1}.

After that, we can state the following lemma concerning to the decomposition of curvature tensor.

Lemma 5.10

(
[
50
]
, Lemma 5.8)
.

Suppose
f
satisfies

\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}f=F.

Let
(t,x^{\prime},\phi(t,x^{\prime}))
denote the parametrization of
\Sigma
, and for
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 2
, let
/\kern-5.50003pt\partial_{\alpha}
denote differentiation along
\Sigma
in the induced coordinates. Then, for
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 2
, one can write

/\kern-5.50003pt\partial_{\alpha}/\kern-5.50003pt\partial_{\beta}(f|_{\Sigma})=l(f_{2})+f_{1},

where

\|f_{2}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}+\|f_{1}\|_{L^{1}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\|df\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s_{0}-1}}+\|df\|_{L^{2}_{t}L_{x}^{\infty}}+\|F\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x}}+\|F\|_{L^{1}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}.

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
R
be the Riemann curvature tensor of the metric
{\mathbf{g}}
. Let
e_{0}=l
. Then for any
0\leq a,b,c,d\leq 2
, we can write

\left<R(e_{a},e_{b})e_{c},e_{d}\right>_{\mathbf{g}}|_{\Sigma}=l(f_{2})+f_{1},

(5.67)

where
|f_{1}|\lesssim|\mathbf{W}|+|d\mathbf{g}|^{2}
and
|f_{2}|\lesssim|d{\mathbf{g}}|
. Moreover, the characteristic energy estimates

\|f_{2}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}+\|f_{1}\|_{L^{1}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\epsilon_{2},

(5.68)

holds. Additionally, for any
t\in[-2,2]
,

\|f_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\lesssim\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

(5.69)

Proof.

According to the expression of curvature tensor, we have

\left<R(e_{a},e_{b})e_{c},e_{d}\right>_{\mathbf{g}}=R_{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}e^{\alpha}_{a}e^{\beta}_{b}e_{c}^{\mu}e_{d}^{\nu},

where

R_{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\left[\partial^{2}_{\alpha\mu}\mathbf{g}_{\beta\nu}+\partial^{2}_{\beta\nu}\mathbf{g}_{\alpha\mu}-\partial^{2}_{\beta\mu}\mathbf{g}_{\alpha\nu}-\partial^{2}_{\alpha\nu}\mathbf{g}_{\beta\mu}\right]+\Theta(\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta},d\mathbf{g}_{\alpha\beta}),

where
\Theta
is a sum of products of coefficients of
\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}
with quadratic forms in
d\mathbf{g}_{\alpha\beta}
. By using Proposition
5.6
, then
\Theta
satisfies the bound required of
f_{1}
. It is sufficient to consider

\frac{1}{2}e^{\alpha}_{a}e^{\beta}_{b}e_{c}^{\mu}e_{d}^{\nu}\left[\partial^{2}_{\alpha\mu}\mathbf{g}_{\beta\nu}+\partial^{2}_{\beta\nu}\mathbf{g}_{\alpha\mu}-\partial^{2}_{\beta\mu}\mathbf{g}_{\alpha\nu}-\partial^{2}_{\alpha\nu}\mathbf{g}_{\beta\mu}\right].

We therefore look at the term
e^{\alpha}_{a}e_{c}^{\mu}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\mu}\mathbf{g}_{\beta\nu}
, which is typical. By (
5.5
), Proposition
5.6
, and Lemma
5.7
, we get

|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|l^{\alpha}-\delta^{\alpha 0}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0},2,\Sigma}+|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\underline{l}^{\alpha}+\delta^{\alpha 0}-2\delta^{\alpha 3}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0},2,\Sigma}+|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|e^{\alpha}_{a}-\delta^{\alpha a}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0},2,\Sigma}\lesssim\epsilon_{1}.

(5.70)

By (
5.70
) and Proposition
5.6
, the term
e_{a}(e_{c}^{\mu})\partial_{\mu}\mathbf{g}_{\beta\nu}
satisfies the bound required of
f_{1}
, so we consider
e_{a}(e_{c}(\mathbf{g}_{\beta\nu}))
. Finally, since the coefficients of
e_{c}
in the basis
/\kern-5.50003pt\partial_{\alpha}
have tangential derivatives bounded in
L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)
, we are reduced by Lemma
5.10
to verifying that

\|\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{g}_{\mu\nu}\|_{L^{1}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}.

Note

\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{g}_{\mu\nu}=\square_{\mathbf{g}}\mathbf{g}_{\mu\nu}.

By Lemma
5.8
, Corollary
5.4
, (
4.66
), and (
4.69
), so it yields

\begin{split}\|\square_{\mathbf{g}}\mathbf{g}_{\mu\nu}\|_{L^{1}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}\lesssim&\ \|\square_{\mathbf{g}}\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}+\|\square_{\mathbf{g}}h\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}\\
\lesssim&\ \|\mathbf{W}\|_{L^{1}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}+\|d{\mathbf{g}}\cdot d{\mathbf{g}}\|_{L^{1}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}\\
\lesssim&\ \|\mathbf{W}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}+\|d{\mathbf{g}}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\|d{\mathbf{g}}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}\\
\lesssim&\ \epsilon_{2}.\end{split}

Above,
\mathbf{W}
and
(d{\mathbf{g}})^{2}
are included in
f_{1}
. We hence complete the proof of Corollary
5.11
.
∎

5.3.2.
Estimate of the connection coefficients

Define

\chi_{ab}=\left<D_{e_{a}}l,e_{b}\right>_{\mathbf{g}},\qquad l(\ln\sigma)=\frac{1}{2}\left<D_{l}\underline{l},l\right>_{\mathbf{g}},\qquad\mu_{0ab}=\left<D_{l}e_{a},e_{b}\right>_{\mathbf{g}}.

(5.71)

For
\sigma
, we set the initial data
\sigma=1
at the time
-2
. Due to Proposition
5.6
, we have

\|\chi_{ab}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}+\|l(\ln\sigma)\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}+\|\mu_{0ab}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\epsilon_{1}.

(5.72)

In a similar way, if we expand
l=l^{\alpha}/\kern-5.50003pt\partial_{\alpha}
in the tangent frame
\partial_{t},\partial_{x^{\prime}}
on
\Sigma
, we infer

l^{0}=1,\quad\|l^{1}\|_{s_{0},2,\Sigma}\lesssim\epsilon_{1}.

(5.73)

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
\chi_{ab}
(
a,b=1,2
) be defined as (
5.71
). Then

\|\chi_{ab}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}.

(5.74)

Furthermore, for any
t\in[-2,2]
,

\|\chi_{ab}\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}+\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Proof.

For
\chi_{ab}
, we know

l(\chi_{ab})=\left<R(l,e_{a})l,e_{b}\right>_{\mathbf{g}}-\chi_{ac}\chi_{cb}-l(\ln\sigma)\chi_{ab}+\mu_{0ab}\chi_{cb}+\mu_{0bc}\chi_{ac},

which is introduced by Klainerman-Rodnianski
[
31
]
. Due to Corollary
5.11
, we can rewrite the above equation as

l(\chi_{ab}-f_{2})=f_{1}-\chi_{ac}\chi_{cb}-l(\ln\sigma)\chi_{ab}+\mu_{0ab}\chi_{cb}+\mu_{0bc}\chi_{ac},

(5.75)

where
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
is the same as in Corollary
5.11
. Let
\Lambda_{x^{\prime}}^{s_{0}-1}
be the fractional derivative operator in the
x^{\prime}
variables. Set

\Psi=f_{1}-\chi_{ac}\chi_{cb}-l(\ln\sigma)\chi_{ab}+\mu_{0ab}\chi_{cb}+\mu_{0bc}\chi_{ac}.

By (
5.75
), then we have

\begin{split}&\|\Lambda_{x^{\prime}}^{s_{0}-1}(\chi_{ab}-f_{2})(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\lesssim\|[\Lambda_{x^{\prime}}^{s_{0}-1},l](\chi_{ab}-f_{2})\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}+\|\Lambda_{x^{\prime}}^{s_{0}-1}\Psi\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}.\end{split}

(5.76)

Note that
H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})
is an algebra for
s_{0}>2
. By direct calculations, we can compute out

\begin{split}\|\Lambda_{x^{\prime}}^{s-1}\Psi\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}&\lesssim\|f_{1}\|_{L^{1}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}+\|\chi\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\\
&\quad+\|\chi\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\cdot\|l(\ln\sigma)\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\\
&\quad+\|\mu\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\cdot\|\chi\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}.\end{split}

(5.77)

We next bound

\displaystyle\|[\Lambda_{x^{\prime}}^{s_{0}-1},l](\chi_{ab}-f_{2})\|_{L^{2}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}

\displaystyle\leq\|/\kern-5.50003pt\partial_{\alpha}l^{\alpha}(\chi_{ab}-f_{2})(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}

\displaystyle\quad\ +\|[\Lambda_{x^{\prime}}^{s_{0}-1}/\kern-5.50003pt\partial_{\alpha},l^{\alpha}](\chi-f_{2})(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}.

By Kato-Ponce commutator estimate and Sobolev embeddings, the above is bounded by

\|l^{1}(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\|\Lambda_{x^{\prime}}^{s_{0}-1}(\chi_{ab}-f_{2})(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}.

(5.78)

Gathering (
5.72
), (
5.73
), (
5.68
), (
5.76
), (
5.77
), and (
5.78
) together, we thus prove that

\sup_{t\in[-2,2]}\|(\chi_{ab}-f_{2})(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}.

This bound combining with (
5.68
) give us (
5.74
).

From (
5.75
), we see that

\begin{split}\|\chi_{ab}-f_{2}\|_{C^{\delta}_{x^{\prime}}}&\lesssim\|f_{1}\|_{L^{1}_{t}C^{\delta}_{x^{\prime}}}+\|\chi_{ac}\chi_{cb}\|_{L^{1}_{t}C^{\delta}_{x^{\prime}}}+\|l(\ln\sigma)\chi_{ab}\|_{L^{1}_{t}C^{\delta}_{x^{\prime}}}\\
&\quad+\|\mu_{0ab}\chi_{cb}\|_{L^{1}_{t}C^{\delta}_{x^{\prime}}}+\|\mu_{0bc}\chi_{ac}\|_{L^{1}_{t}C^{\delta}_{x^{\prime}}}.\end{split}

(5.79)

Seeing (
5.79
), using (
5.68
), (
5.69
), (
5.72
), together with Sobolev imbedding
15
15

15

The number
\delta_{0}
is defined in (
1.35
) and
\delta_{0}\in(0,s_{0}-2)
.

H^{s_{0}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow C^{\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we derive that

\|\chi_{ab}\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}+\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Therefore, we complete the proof of Lemma
5.12
.
∎

5.3.3.
Proof of Proposition
5.9

Our goal here is to prove (
5.62
) and (
5.63
). We recall

\Re(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})=|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|d\phi(t,x^{\prime})-dt|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0},2,\Sigma}.

Combining (
5.66
), with Proposition
5.6
, then (
5.62
) follows from the bound

|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|l-(\partial_{t}-\partial_{x_{3}})|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0},2,\Sigma}\lesssim\epsilon_{2},

where it is understood that one takes the norm of the coefficients of
l-(\partial_{t}-\partial_{x_{3}})
in the standard frame on
\mathbb{R}^{3+1}
. Let
\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta\gamma}
be the Christoffel symbols. Then we have

\|\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta\gamma}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|d{\mathbf{g}}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}

(5.80)

Due to (
5.80
) and the geodesic equation, we infer

\|l-(\partial_{t}-\partial_{x_{3}})\|_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}.

(5.81)

So it’s sufficient to bound the tangential derivatives of the coefficients of
l-(\partial_{t}-\partial_{x_{3}})
in the norm
L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma)
. By using Proposition
5.6
, we can estimate the Christoffel symbols

\|\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta\gamma}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}.

(5.82)

For
s_{0}>2
, then
H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})
is an algebra. By (
5.80
), (
5.81
), (
5.82
), and Proposition
5.6
, we get

\|\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta\gamma}e_{1}^{\beta}l^{\gamma}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}.

We now establish the following bound,

\|\left<D_{e_{a}}l,e_{b}\right>\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}+\|\left<D_{e_{a}}l,\underline{l}\right>\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}+\|\left<D_{l}l,\underline{l}\right>\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{s_{0}-1}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}.

(5.83)

The first term in (
5.83
) is
\chi_{ab}
, which has been bounded by Lemma
5.12
. For the second term in (
5.83
), noting

\left<D_{e_{a}}l,\underline{l}\right>=\left<D_{e_{a}}l,2\partial_{t}\right>=-2\left<D_{e_{a}}\partial_{t},l\right>,

so it can be bounded via Proposition
5.6
. In a similar idea, the last term in (
5.83
) can also be controlled by Proposition
5.6
.

It still remains for us to prove

\|d\phi(t,x^{\prime})-dt\|_{C^{1,{\delta_{0}}}_{x^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}+\|d\mathbf{g}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

By (
5.66
), it is sufficient to show that

\|l(t,\cdot)-(\partial_{t}-\partial_{x_{3}})\|_{C^{1,{\delta_{0}}}_{x^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}+\|d\mathbf{g}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

The coefficients of
e_{1}
are small in
C^{\delta_{0}}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})
perturbations of their constant coefficient analogs, so it is sufficient to show that

\|\left<D_{e_{1}}l,e_{1}\right>(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}+\|\left<D_{e_{1}}l,\underline{l}\right>(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}+\|d\mathbf{g}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Above, the first term is bounded by Lemma
5.12
, and the second term can be estimated by using

\|\left<D_{e_{1}}\partial_{t},l\right>(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{t})}\lesssim\|d\mathbf{g}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{\delta_{0}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Therefore, we complete the proof of Proposition
5.9
.

5.4.
Strichartz estimates of solutions

Our goal is to prove the Strichartz estimates in Proposition
4.3
. We start to introduce a proposition as follows.

Proposition 5.13
.

Suppose that
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\in\mathcal{{H}}
and
\Re(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
. Let
\mathbf{g}
be denoted in (
4.65
). For each
1\leq r\leq s+1
, and for each
t_{0}\in[-2,2]
, then the linear, homogeneous equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}f=0,\qquad(t,x)\in[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&(f,\partial_{t}f)|_{t=t_{0}}=(f_{0},f_{1}),\end{cases}

(5.84)

is well-posed on the time-interval
[-2,2]
if the data
(f_{0},f_{1})\in H_{x}^{r}\times H_{x}^{r-1}
. Moreover, the solution satisfies, respectively, the energy estimates

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r}}+\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r-1}}\leq C\big(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}\big),

and the Strichartz estimates
16
16

16

here the constant
C
is universal if the solution
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\in\mathcal{{H}}
.

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{k}f\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C\big(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}\big),\quad k<r-1,

(5.85)

hold. The similar estimates also hold when we replace
\left<\nabla\right>^{k}
by
\left<\nabla\right>^{k-1}d
.

Remark 5.1
.

The proof for Proposition
5.13
can be found in section
6
.

Due to Proposition
5.13
, we next introduce a result for Strichartz estimates of inhomogeneous waves.

Corollary 5.14
.

Suppose that
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\in\mathcal{{H}}
and
\Re(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
. Let
\mathbf{g}
is denoted in (
4.65
). For each
1\leq r\leq s+1
, and for each
t_{0}\in[-2,2]
, then the linear wave equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}f=F,\qquad(t,x)\in[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&(f,\partial_{t}f)|_{t=t_{0}}=(f_{0},f_{1}),\end{cases}

(5.86)

is well-posed on the time-interval
[-2,2]
if the initial data
(f_{0},f_{1})\in H_{x}^{r}\times H_{x}^{r-1}
. Furthermore, the solution satisfies energy estimates

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r}}+\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r-1}}\leq C\big(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r-1}}\big),

(5.87)

and the Strichartz estimates

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{k}f\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C\big(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r-1}}\big),\quad k<r-1,

(5.88)

and the similar estimates also hold when we replace
\left<\nabla\right>^{k}
by
\left<\nabla\right>^{k-1}d
. Additionally, if
2<r\leq s
, we have

\|df\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}\dot{B}^{0}_{\infty,2}}\leq C\big(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r-1}}\big).

(5.89)

Proof.

By using Proposition
5.13
and Duhamel’s principle, we can obtain (
5.87
) and (
5.88
) directly. To prove (
5.89
), operating
P_{j}
on (
5.86
), it follows

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}P_{j}f=p_{j}F+[\square_{\mathbf{g}},P_{j}]f,\\
&(P_{j}f,\partial_{t}P_{j}f)|_{t=t_{0}}=(P_{j}f_{0},P_{j}f_{1}).\end{cases}

(5.90)

For
r>2
, by using (
5.88
) on (
5.90
), we therefore get

\begin{split}\|P_{j}df\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim&\|P_{j}f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|P_{j}f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}\\
&+\|P_{j}F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|[\square_{\mathbf{g}},P_{j}]f\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r-1}}.\end{split}

(5.91)

Taking the
l^{2}
sum on (
5.91
) for
j
, and using Lemma
2.11
, then we derive that

\begin{split}\|df\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}\dot{B}^{0}_{\infty,2}}\lesssim&\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r-1}}\\
&+\|df\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L_{x}^{\infty}}\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}{H}_{x}^{r-1}}+\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L_{x}^{\infty}}\|df\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}{H}_{x}^{r-1}}\\
\lesssim&\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r-1}}.\end{split}

Above, we also use (
5.7
), (
5.87
), and
2<r\leq s
.
∎

We also need the following Strichartz estimate:

Proposition 5.15
.

Suppose that
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\in\mathcal{{H}}
and
\Re(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
. Let
\mathbf{g}
is denoted in (
4.65
).
For any
1\leq r\leq s+1
, and for each
t_{0}\in[-2,2]
, the linear, non-homogeneous equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}f=\mathbf{g}^{0\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}F,\qquad(t,x)\in(t_{0},2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&(f,\partial_{t}f)|_{t=t_{0}}=(f_{0},F(t_{0},\cdot)),\end{cases}

admits a solution
f\in C([-2,2],H_{x}^{r})\times C^{1}([-2,2],H_{x}^{r-1})
and the following estimates holds:

\begin{split}\|f\|_{L_{[-2,2]}^{\infty}H_{x}^{r}}+\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L_{[-2,2]}^{\infty}H_{x}^{r-1}}\lesssim&\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r}},\end{split}

(5.92)

and provided
k<r-1
,

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{k}f\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r}},

(5.93)

and the similar estimates also hold when we repace
\left<\nabla\right>^{k}
by
\left<\nabla\right>^{k-1}d
. Additionally, if
2<r\leq s
, we have

\|df\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}\dot{B}^{0}_{\infty,2}}\leq C\big(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r}}\big).

(5.94)

Proof.

Let
V
satisfy the the linear, homogenous equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}V=0,\\
&V(t,x)|_{t=t_{0}}=f_{0},\quad\partial_{t}V(t,x)|_{t=t_{0}}=0,\end{cases}

(5.95)

Let
K
satisfy the the linear, nonhomogenous equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}K=\mathbf{g}^{0\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}F,\\
&K(t,x)|_{t=t_{0}}=0,\quad\partial_{t}K(t,x)|_{t=t_{0}}=F(t_{0},x),\end{cases}

(5.96)

By the superposition principle, and referring (
5.95
) and (
5.96
), then
f=V+K
satisfies

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}f=\mathbf{g}^{0\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}F,\\
&(f,\partial_{t}f)|_{t=t_{0}}=(f_{0},F(t_{0},x)).\end{cases}

Due to Proposition
5.13
, it follows

\|V\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r}}+\|\partial_{t}V\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r-1}}\leq C\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}},

(5.97)

and

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{k}V\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}},\quad k<r-1.

(5.98)

Using Corollary
5.14
, we have

\|dV\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}\dot{B}^{0}_{\infty,2}}\leq C\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}},\quad 2<r\leq s.

(5.99)

By Lemma
2.13
and Proposition
5.13
, we can bound
K
by

\|K\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r}}+\|\partial_{t}K\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r-1}}\leq C\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r}},

(5.100)

and

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{k}K\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r}},\quad k<r-1.

(5.101)

Using Corollary
5.14
again, we can obtain

\|dV\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}\dot{B}^{0}_{\infty,2}}\leq C\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r}},\quad 2<r\leq s.

(5.102)

Adding (
5.97
) and (
5.100
), we get (
5.92
). Adding (
5.98
) and (
5.101
), we get (
5.93
). Adding (
5.99
) and (
5.102
), we get (
5.94
). At this stage, we have finished the proof of Proposition
5.15
.
∎

Based on Proposition
5.13
and
5.15
, we can obtain the Strichartz estimates for the solutions of (
1.13
) as follows.

Proposition 5.16
.

Suppose
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\in\mathcal{H}
and
\Re(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
. Let
2<s_{0}<s\leq\frac{5}{2}
. Let
\mathbf{u}_{+}
be defined in (
1.23
). Then for
k<s-1
, we have

\displaystyle\|(\left<\nabla\right>^{k}h,\left<\nabla\right>^{k-1}\mathbf{u}_{+})\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}\dot{B}^{0}_{\infty,2}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\|(u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}},h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+}},

(5.103)

and the similar estimates hold if we replace
\left<\nabla\right>^{k}
with
\left<\nabla\right>^{k-1}d
.

Proof.

By (
1.20
) and (
2.68
), we can obtain

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}h=D,\\
&\square_{\mathbf{g}}\mathbf{u}_{+}=\mathbf{g}^{0\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\mathbf{Z}+\mathbf{Q}+\mathbf{B}.\end{cases}

(5.104)

By using (
1.21
), (
2.70
), (
1.22
), Lemma
2.2
, and (
5.7
), we therefore get

\begin{split}\|D\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s-1}}+\|\mathbf{Q}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s-1}}\lesssim&\|(d\mathbf{u},dh)\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L_{x}^{\infty}}\|(d\mathbf{u},dh)\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s-1}},\\
\lesssim&\|(d\mathbf{u},dh)\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L_{x}^{\infty}}\|(u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}},h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}\\
\lesssim&\|(u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}},h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}.\end{split}

(5.105)

By using (
2.70
) and Lemma
2.2
, it yields

\begin{split}\|\mathbf{B}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s-1}}\lesssim&\|\mathbf{T}\mathbf{u}_{-}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|(d\mathbf{u}_{-},d\mathbf{u},dh)\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L_{x}^{\infty}}\|(d\mathbf{u},d\mathbf{u}_{-},dh)\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s-1}}.\end{split}

(5.106)

Using (
1.24
) and (
5.7
), it follows

\begin{split}\|\mathbf{u}_{-}\|_{H^{\frac{5}{2}+}_{x}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{w}\|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}+}_{x}}(1+\|h\|_{H^{s}_{x}})\lesssim\|\mathbf{w}\|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}+}_{x}}.\end{split}

(5.107)

Due to (
2.84
) and (
5.7
), we can compute out

\begin{split}\|\mathbf{T}\mathbf{u}_{-}\|_{H_{x}^{s}}\lesssim&\|\mathbf{w}\|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}+}_{x}}(\|(u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}},h)\|_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|(u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}},h)\|^{2}_{H_{x}^{s}})\\
\lesssim&\|\mathbf{w}\|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}+}_{x}}.\end{split}

(5.108)

From (
2.69
), so we get

\begin{split}\|\mathbf{Z}\|_{H_{x}^{s}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{T}\mathbf{u}_{-}\|_{H_{x}^{s}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{w}\|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}+}_{x}}.\end{split}

(5.109)

Combing (
5.108
), (
5.107
), (
5.106
), and using (
5.7
), we have

\begin{split}\|\mathbf{B}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s-1}}\lesssim&\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}H^{\frac{3}{2}+}_{x}}(1+\|(u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}},h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|(u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}},h)\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}})\\
&+\|(d\mathbf{u},dh)\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L_{x}^{\infty}}\|(u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}},h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}\\
\lesssim&\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}H^{\frac{3}{2}+}_{x}}+\|(u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}},h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}.\end{split}

(5.110)

Using Propostion
5.14
and
5.15
for Equation (
5.104
), combining the bounds (
5.105
), (
5.109
), and (
5.110
), we can prove (
5.103
). At this stage, we have finished the proof of Proposition
5.16
.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

We expect that Proposition
5.16
yields a Strichartz estimate of solutions with sharp regularity of the velocity, enthalpy, and vorticity.

Proposition 5.17
.

Suppose
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\in\mathcal{H}
and
\Re(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
. Let
2<s_{0}<s<\frac{5}{2}
and
\delta\in[0,s-2)
. Let
\mathbf{u}_{+}
be defined in (
1.23
)-(
1.24
). Then we have the following Strichartz estimates

\|d\mathbf{u},dh,d\mathbf{u}_{+}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}C^{\delta}_{x}}+\|d\mathbf{u}_{+},d\mathbf{u},dh\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}}\leq\epsilon_{2},

(5.111)

and the energy estimates

\|(h,u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}})\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s_{0}}}\leq\epsilon_{2}.

(5.112)

Proof.

Taking
k=s_{0}-1
in Proposition
5.16
, we then obtain

\begin{split}\|dh,d\mathbf{u}_{+}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}}\lesssim&\|(u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}},h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+}}.\end{split}

(5.113)

Using Proposition
5.16
again, for
\delta\in[0,s-2)
, we shall get

\begin{split}\|dh,d\mathbf{u}_{+}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}C^{\delta}_{x}}\lesssim&\|(u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}},h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+}}.\end{split}

(5.114)

Due to (
1.24
) and (
5.7
), it follows

\begin{split}\|\nabla\mathbf{u}_{-}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}}\lesssim&\|\nabla\mathbf{u}_{-}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}H^{s_{0}-\frac{1}{2}}_{x}}\\
\lesssim&\|\mathrm{e}^{-h}\mathbf{W}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}H^{s_{0}-\frac{3}{2}}_{x}}\\
\lesssim&\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}H^{s_{0}}_{x}}(1+\|h\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H^{s}_{x}})\\
\lesssim&\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}H^{s_{0}}_{x}}.\end{split}

(5.115)

By using Theorem
3.2
and
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\in\mathcal{H}
, it follows that

\begin{split}\|(u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}},h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s_{0}}}\leq&C(\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon^{2}_{3})\exp\left\{C(\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon^{2}_{3})\epsilon_{2}\mathrm{e}^{C(\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon^{2}_{3})\epsilon_{2}}\right\}\\
\leq&\epsilon_{2}.\end{split}

(5.116)

Therefore, by (
5.116
), (
5.114
), (
5.108
), (
5.115
), and (
1.36
), it turns out

\begin{split}\|dh,d\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}}\leq&C(\|(u^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}},h)\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s_{0}}})\\
\leq&C(\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon^{2}_{3})\exp\left\{C(\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon^{2}_{3})\epsilon_{2}\mathrm{e}^{C(\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon^{2}_{3})\epsilon_{2}}\right\}\\
\leq&\epsilon_{2}.\end{split}

(5.117)

Similarly, we can also prove that

\begin{split}\|dh,d\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}C^{\delta}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}.\end{split}

(5.118)

To summarize (
5.114
), (
5.116
), (
5.117
) and (
5.118
), we have finished the proof of Proposition
5.17
.
∎

6.
Strichartz estimate for linear waves

In this part, our goal is to prove Proposition
5.13
. Following Smith-Tataru’s paper
[
50
]
, we give a proof for Proposition
5.13
. The method relies on a wave-packet construction for the metric
\mathbf{g}
in (
4.65
). We mention that the use of wave-packets is introduced by Smith
[
47
]
, and developed by Wolff
[
57
]
and Smith-Tataru
[
50
]
. Following this idea, so we can represent approximate solutions to the linear equation as a square summable superposition of wave packets, and the wave packets are localized in phase space. As a result, we can obtain Strichartz estimates of linear wave equations with sufficient regularity of the foliations.

We also set
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\in\mathcal{{H}}
and
\Re\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
, where
\mathcal{{H}}
and
\Re
are defined in (
4.67
)-(
4.69
) and (
5.4
) respectively. Then
\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq\epsilon_{0}
and
\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}C^{\delta}_{x}}\leq\epsilon_{0}
, where
\epsilon_{0}
has been stated in (
1.36
).

Let
P_{\lambda}
be the Littlewood-Paley operator with frequency
\lambda
. For
\lambda\geq 1
, we consider the smooth metric

\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda}P_{\lambda^{\prime}}\mathbf{g}.

6.1.
Proof of Proposition
5.13

We first state the following result in a phase space.

Proposition 6.1
.

Suppose that
(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\in\mathcal{{H}}
and
\Re(h,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
. Suppose
f
satisfy

\begin{cases}\square_{\mathbf{g}}f=0,\quad(t,x)\in[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
(f,\partial_{t}f)|_{t=t_{0}}=(f_{0},f_{1}).\end{cases}

(6.1)

Then for each
(f_{0},f_{1})\in H^{1}\times L^{2}
there exists a function
f_{\lambda}\in C^{\infty}([-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
, with

\mathrm{supp}\widehat{f_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)}\subseteq\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\frac{\lambda}{8}\leq|\xi|\leq 8\lambda\},

such that

\begin{cases}&\|\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}f_{\lambda}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}(\|{P_{\lambda}}f_{0}\|_{H^{1}}+\|{P_{\lambda}}f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}),\\
&f_{\lambda}(t_{0})=P_{\lambda}f_{0},\quad\partial_{t}f_{\lambda}(t_{0})=P_{\lambda}f_{1}.\end{cases}

(6.2)

Additionally, the following Strichartz estimates holds:

\|f_{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\ln\lambda)^{\frac{1}{2}}(\|{P_{\lambda}}f_{0}\|_{H^{1}}+\|{P_{\lambda}}f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}).

(6.3)

Remark 6.1
.

In fact, Proposition
6.1
tells us we can find a good approximate solution
f_{\lambda}
for the problem

\begin{cases}\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}f_{\lambda}=0,\quad(t,x)\in[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
(f_{\lambda},\partial_{t}f_{\lambda})|_{t=t_{0}}=(P_{\lambda}f_{0},P_{\lambda}f_{1}).\end{cases}

For
\epsilon_{0}\lambda\leq 1
, the result in Proposition
6.1
is almost trivial. Indeed, we can set
f_{\lambda}=P_{\lambda}f
, where
f
is the exact solution of

\begin{cases}\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}f=0,\quad(t,x)\in[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
(f,\partial_{t}f)|_{t=t_{0}}=(P_{\lambda}f_{0},P_{\lambda}f_{1}).\end{cases}

(6.4)

Using energy estimates for (
6.4
), we can see

\|df\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\|P_{\lambda}f_{0}\|_{H^{1}}+\|P_{\lambda}f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}.

(6.5)

Moreover, for
\mathbf{g}^{00}_{\lambda}=-1
, so we have

\begin{split}\|\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}f_{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim&\|[\mathbf{g}^{\alpha i}_{\lambda},\partial_{\alpha}P_{\lambda}]\partial_{i}f\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}+\|P_{\lambda}(\partial_{\alpha}\mathbf{g}^{\alpha i}_{\lambda})\partial_{i}f\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\\
\lesssim&\|d\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\|d{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\\
\lesssim&\epsilon_{0}(\|P_{\lambda}f_{0}\|_{H^{1}}+\|P_{\lambda}f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}).\end{split}

The Strichartz estimate (
6.3
) follows from Sobolev imbeddings and (
6.5
). Hence, we will restrict to establishing Proposition
6.1
in the case that

\epsilon_{0}\lambda\gg 1.

Based on Proposition
6.1
, we can prove Proposition
5.13
as follows.

Proof of Proposition
5.13
by using Proposition
6.1
.

We first prove the case of
r=1
by using Proposition
6.1
. Based on the result for
r=1
, combining with Kato-Ponce commutator estimates and Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality, we are able to give a proof for other case
1<r\leq 1+s
.

Step 1:
The case
r=1
. Using basic energy estimates for (
5.84
), we have

\begin{split}\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim&\ (\|f_{0}\|_{H^{1}}+\|f_{1}\|_{L^{2}})\exp(\int^{t}_{0}\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau)\\
\lesssim&\ \|f_{0}\|_{H^{1}}+\|f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

Then the Cauchy problem (
1.41
) holds a unique solution
f\in C([-2,2],H_{x}^{1})
and
\partial_{t}f\in C([-2,2],L_{x}^{2})
. It remains to show that the solution
f
also satisfies the Strichartz estimate (
5.85
).

Without loss of generality, we take
t_{0}=0
in Proposition
5.13
. For any given initial data
(f_{0},f_{1})\in H^{1}\times L^{2}
, and
t_{0}\in[-2,2]
, we take a Littlewood-Paley decomposition

f_{0}=\textstyle{\sum}_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}f_{0},\qquad f_{1}=\textstyle{\sum}_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}f_{1},

and for each
\lambda
we take the corresponding
f_{\lambda}
as in Proposition
6.1
. If we set

f^{*}=\textstyle{\sum}_{\lambda}f_{\lambda},

(6.6)

then
f^{*}
matches the initial data
(f_{0},f_{1})
at the time
t=t_{0}
, and also satisfies the Strichartz estimates (
6.3
). We claim that
f^{*}
is also an approximate solution for
\square_{\mathbf{g}}
in the sense that

\|\square_{\mathbf{g}}f^{*}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}(\|f_{0}\|_{H^{1}}+\|f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}).

(6.7)

We can derive (
6.7
) by using the decomposition

\square_{\mathbf{g}}f^{*}=\textstyle{\sum_{\lambda}}\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}f_{\lambda}+\textstyle{\sum_{\lambda}}\square_{\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}f_{\lambda}.

(6.8)

In (
6.8
), the first one
\textstyle{\sum_{\lambda}}\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}f_{\lambda}
can be controlled by Proposition
6.1
. As for the second one
\textstyle{\sum_{\lambda}}\square_{\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}f_{\lambda}
, noting
\mathbf{g}^{00}=-1
, we then have

\begin{split}\textstyle{\sum_{\lambda}}\square_{\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}f_{\lambda}=\textstyle{\sum_{\lambda}}({\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}})\nabla df_{\lambda}.\end{split}

Due to Hölder inequality, we obtain

\begin{split}\|\textstyle{\sum_{\lambda}}\square_{\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}f_{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\mathrm{sup}_{\lambda}\left(\lambda\|{\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\right)\left(\sum_{\lambda}\|df_{\lambda}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

(6.9)

On the other hand, we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{sup}_{\lambda}\left(\lambda\|{\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\right)\lesssim&\ \mathrm{sup}_{\lambda}\big(\lambda\sum_{\mu>\lambda}\|\mathbf{g}_{\mu}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big)\\
\lesssim&\ \mathrm{sup}_{\lambda}\big(\lambda\sum_{\mu>\lambda}\mu^{-(1+\delta)}\|d\mathbf{g}_{\mu}\|_{C^{\delta}_{x}}\big)\\
\lesssim&\ \|d\mathbf{g}\|_{C^{\delta}_{x}}(\textstyle{\sum_{\mu}}\mu^{-\delta})\lesssim\ \|d\mathbf{g}\|_{C^{\delta}_{x}}.\end{split}

(6.10)

For
f_{\lambda}
is an approximate solution for the problem

\begin{cases}\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}v=0,\quad(t,x)\in[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
(v,\partial_{t}v)|_{t=t_{0}}=(P_{\lambda}f_{0},P_{\lambda}f_{1}).\end{cases}

Due to energy estimate and (
6.2
), it yields

\begin{split}\|df_{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim&\|P_{\lambda}f_{0}\|_{H^{1}}+\|P_{\lambda}f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}f_{\lambda}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\\
\lesssim&\|P_{\lambda}f_{0}\|_{H^{1}}+\|P_{\lambda}f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

(6.11)

Gathering (
6.9
), (
6.10
), and (
6.11
), we can prove that

\begin{split}\|\textstyle{\sum_{\lambda}}\square_{\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}f_{\lambda}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}(\|f_{0}\|_{H^{1}}+\|f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}),\end{split}

where we also use
\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}C^{\delta}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}
. Hence, we have proved (
6.7
). But
f^{*}
is not the exact solution to
\square_{\mathbf{g}}f=0
with the Cauchy data
(f_{0},f_{1})
. So we seek another function
\mathbf{M}F
satisfying

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}\mathbf{M}F=-\square_{\mathbf{g}}f^{*},\\
&(\mathbf{M}F,\partial_{t}\mathbf{M}F)=(0,0).\end{cases}

(6.12)

For given
F=-\square_{\mathbf{g}}f^{*}\in L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}
, we now form the function

F^{(1)}=\int^{t}_{0}f^{(1)}(\tau;t,x)d\tau,

where
f^{(1)}(\tau;t,x)
is the approximate solution of
\square_{\mathbf{g}}\tilde{f}=0
and
f^{(1)}
is formed like
f^{*}
(cf. (
6.6
)) with the Cauchy data

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}f^{(1)}=0,\qquad(t,x)\in(\tau,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&f^{(1)}(\tau,t,x)|_{t=\tau}=0,\quad\partial_{t}f^{(1)}(\tau,t,x)|_{t=\tau}=F(\tau,\cdot).\end{cases}

By calculating

\begin{split}\partial_{t}F^{(1)}=&\int^{t}_{0}\partial_{t}f^{(1)}(\tau;t,x)d\tau+f^{(1)}(t;t,x)=\int^{t}_{0}\partial_{t}f^{(1)}(\tau;t,x)d\tau,\\
\partial_{tt}F^{(1)}=&\int^{t}_{0}\partial_{tt}f^{(1)}(\tau;t,x)d\tau+\partial_{t}f^{(1)}(\tau;t,x)=\int^{t}_{0}\partial_{tt}f^{(1)}(t;t,x)d\tau+F(t,x),\\
\partial_{ti}F^{(1)}=&\int^{t}_{0}\partial_{ti}f^{(1)}(t;t,x)d\tau,\quad i=1,2,3,\\
\partial_{ij}F^{(1)}=&\int^{t}_{0}\partial_{ij}f^{(1)}(t;t,x)d\tau,\quad i,j=1,2,3,\end{split}

we then have

\square_{\mathbf{g}}F^{(1)}=\int^{t}_{0}\square_{\mathbf{g}}f^{(1)}(\tau;t,x)d\tau+F.

By using (
6.7
), we derive that

\|\square_{\mathbf{g}}F^{(1)}-F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\|\square_{\mathbf{g}}f^{(1)}(\tau;t,x)\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}.

For
F^{(m)},m\geq 2
, we perform an iteration as follows. Let

F^{(2)}=\int^{t}_{0}f^{(2)}(\tau;t,x)d\tau,

where
f^{(2)}(\tau;t,x)
is the approximate solution of
\square_{\mathbf{g}}\tilde{f}=0
and
f^{(2)}
is formed like
f^{*}
(cf. (
6.6
)) with Cauchy data

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}f^{(2)}=0,\qquad(t,\mathbf{x})\in(\tau,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&f^{(2)}(\tau;t,x)|_{t=\tau}=0,\quad\partial_{t}f^{(2)}(\tau;t,x)|_{t=\tau}=F-\square_{\mathbf{g}}F^{(1)}.\end{cases}

Then we can prove that

\|\square_{\mathbf{g}}F^{(2)}-(F-\square_{\mathbf{g}}F^{(1)})\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}\|F-\square_{\mathbf{g}}F^{(1)}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon^{2}_{0}\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}.

We rewrite the above equality as

\|\square_{\mathbf{g}}(F^{(2)}+F^{(1)})-F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}\|F-\square_{\mathbf{g}}F^{(1)}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon^{2}_{0}\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}.

For
m\geq 2
, we can set

F^{(m)}=\int^{t}_{0}f^{(m)}(\tau;t,x)d\tau,

where
f^{(m)}(\tau;t,x)
is the approximate solution of
\square_{\mathbf{g}}\tilde{f}=0
and
f^{(m)}
is formed like
f^{*}
(cf. (
6.6
)) with Cauchy data

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}f^{(m)}=0,\qquad(t,x)\in(\tau,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&f^{(m)}(\tau;t,x)|_{t=\tau}=0,\quad\partial_{t}f^{(2)}(\tau;t,x)|_{t=\tau}=F-\square_{\mathbf{g}}(F^{(1)}+F^{(2)}+\cdots F^{(m-1)}).\end{cases}

we therefore obtain that

\|\square_{\mathbf{g}}(F^{(m)}+F^{(m-1)}+\cdots F^{(1)})-F\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon^{m}_{0}\|F\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}.

(6.13)

Seeing (
6.13
), by using the contraction principle, there is a limit

\mathbf{M}F=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}(F^{(m)}+F^{(m-1)}+\cdots F^{(1)}).

Hence, we get

\square_{\mathbf{g}}\mathbf{M}F=-\square_{\mathbf{g}}f^{*},\quad(\mathbf{M}F,\partial_{t}\mathbf{M}F)=(0,0).

(6.14)

Gathering (
6.12
) and (
6.14
), we can write the solution
f
in the form

f=f^{*}+\mathbf{M}F,

where
f^{*}
is the approximation solution formed in (
6.6
) for initial data
(f_{0},f_{1})
specified at time
t=0
, and it satisfies the Strichartz estimates (
6.3
), and

\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}(\|f_{0}\|_{H^{1}}+\|f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}).

The Strichartz estimates of
\mathbf{M}F
now follow since they holds for each
f^{(m)}(\tau;t,x)
,
\tau\in[0,t]
. Therefore, the estimate (
5.85
) holds.

Step 2:
The case
r=2
. We also consider
t_{0}=0
. Given data
(f_{0},f_{1})\in H^{2}\times H^{1}
, we find a solution of the form
f=\left<\nabla\right>^{-1}\bar{f}
. Then we have

\square_{\mathbf{g}}\bar{f}=(\square_{\mathbf{g}}-\left<\nabla\right>\square_{\mathbf{g}}\left<\nabla\right>^{-1})\bar{f}=[\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta},\left<\nabla\right>]\left<\nabla\right>^{-1}\partial_{\alpha\beta}\bar{f}.

Hence,
\bar{f}
solves

\begin{cases}\square_{\mathbf{g}}\bar{f}=[\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta},\left<\nabla\right>]\left<\nabla\right>^{-1}\partial_{\alpha\beta}\bar{f},\quad(t,x)\in[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
\bar{f}|_{t=0}=\left<\nabla\right>f_{0},\quad\partial_{t}\bar{f}|_{t=0}=\left<\nabla\right>f_{1}.\end{cases}

For
\bar{F}=[\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta},\left<\nabla\right>]\left<\nabla\right>^{-1}\partial_{\alpha\beta}\bar{f}\in L_{[-2,2]}^{1}L^{2}_{x}
, we form
\mathbf{M}\bar{F}
as above in Step 1. So we have
\square_{\mathbf{g}}\mathbf{M}\bar{F}=\bar{F}
, and
\mathbf{M}\bar{F}
has vanishing Cauchy data at
t_{0}=0
. Let
\bar{f}^{*}
be the solution for the homogeneous equation

\begin{cases}\square_{\mathbf{g}}\bar{f}^{*}=0,\quad(t,x)\in[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
\bar{f}^{*}|_{t=0}=\left<\nabla\right>f_{0},\quad\partial_{t}\bar{f}^{*}|_{t=0}=\left<\nabla\right>f_{1}.\end{cases}

Then we can seek a solution
\bar{f}
of the form
\bar{f}=\bar{f}^{*}+\mathbf{M}\bar{F}
, provided that

\|[\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta},\left<\nabla\right>]\left<\nabla\right>^{-1}\partial_{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{M}\bar{F}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}\|\bar{F}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}.

(6.15)

Let us prove (
6.15
). Considering
\mathbf{g}^{00}=-1
, by using Coifman-Maeyer estimate, we derive that

\|[\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta},\left<\nabla\right>]\left<\nabla\right>^{-1}\partial_{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{M}\bar{F}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|d\mathbf{M}\bar{F}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}.

(6.16)

By using (
6.14
), we also obtain

\|d\mathbf{M}\bar{F}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\|\bar{F}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}.

(6.17)

Combining (
6.16
) with (
6.17
), and using
\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}
, we can get (
6.15
).

Therefore, by Duhamel’s principle, in the case of
r=1
or
r=2
, we can also obtain the Strichartz estimates for the linear, nonhomogeneous wave equation

\begin{cases}\square_{\mathbf{g}}f=G,\quad(t,x)\in[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
f|_{t=0}=f_{0},\quad\partial_{t}f|_{t=0}=f_{1},\end{cases}

as

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{k}f\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}}+\|G\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r-1}},\quad k<r-1.

(6.18)

Now, we claim that the following bounds
17
17

17

Comparing with (
6.18
), the bound (
6.19
) is stronger, for it includes the time-derivatives of
f
. It plays an important role in the next step.
hold for

and

,

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{k}df\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}}+\|G\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r-1}},\quad k<r-2.

(6.19)

Let us now use (
6.18
) to prove (
6.19
).

To prove (
6.19
) for
r=2
, we first consider
G=0
. That is,

\square_{\mathbf{g}}f=0,(f,\partial_{t}f)|_{t=0}=(f_{0},f_{1})\in H^{2}\times H^{1}
. For
\mathbf{g}^{00}=-1
, we then have

\square_{\mathbf{g}}df=d\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha\beta}f\in L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}.

(6.20)

The right hand side of (
6.20
) can be derived by energy estimates and Gronwall’s inequality. That is

\begin{split}\|\partial_{t}df\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|\nabla df\|_{L^{2}_{x}}&\lesssim(\|f_{0}\|_{H^{2}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{1}})\exp\left(\int^{2}_{0}\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}dt\right)\\
&\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H^{2}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{1}}.\end{split}

Then
d\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha\beta}f\in L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}
holds. Thus, (
6.19
) holds for
r=2
and
G=0
. If
G\neq 0
, we can use the Duhamel principle to handle it. Decompose
f=f^{a}+f^{b}
, where

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}f^{a}=0,\\
&(f^{a},\partial_{t}f^{a})|_{t=0}=(f_{0},f_{1})\in H^{2}\times H^{1},\end{cases}

(6.21)

and

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}f^{b}=G,\\
&(f^{a},\partial_{t}f^{b})|_{t=0}=(0,0).\end{cases}

(6.22)

So we can get the Strichartz estimates for
f^{a}

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{k}df^{a}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}},\quad k<r-2.

For
f^{b}
, we use the iteration process like
\mathbf{M}F
in Step 1, and the following Strichartz estimates for
f^{b}

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{k}df^{b}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|G\|_{L^{1}_{t}H_{x}^{r-1}},\quad k<r-2.

So the conclusion (
6.19
) holds for
f=f^{a}+f^{b}
.

To prove (
6.19
) for
r=1
, we note that,
\left<\nabla\right>^{-1}f
has the Cauchy data in
H^{2}\times H^{1}
if the Cauchy data of
f
is in
H^{1}\times L^{2}
, and

\begin{split}\|\square_{\mathbf{g}}\left<\nabla\right>^{-1}f\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H^{1}_{x}}=&\|\left<\nabla\right>\square_{\mathbf{g}}\left<\nabla\right>^{-1}f\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\\
\lesssim&\|[\left<\nabla\right>,\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}]\left<\nabla\right>^{-1}\partial_{\alpha\beta}f\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}+\|G\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}.\end{split}

Noting
\mathbf{g}^{00}=-1
, then we give the following bounds by Coifman-Meyer estimate and energy estimates for
f

\begin{split}\|[\left<\nabla\right>,\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}]\left<\nabla\right>^{-1}\partial_{\alpha\beta}f\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim&\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\|df\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\\
\lesssim&\epsilon_{0}(\|f_{0}\|_{H^{1}}+\|f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}).\end{split}

Step 3:
The case
1<r<2
. We also set
t_{0}=0
. Based on the above result in Step 1 and Step 2, we also transform the initial data in
H^{1}\times L^{2}
. Applying
\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}
to (
6.1
), we have

\square_{\mathbf{g}}\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}f=-[\square_{\mathbf{g}},\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}]f.

Let
\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}f=\bar{f}
. Then
\bar{f}
is a solution to

\begin{cases}\square_{\mathbf{g}}\bar{f}=-[\square_{\mathbf{g}},\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}]\left<\nabla\right>^{1-r}\bar{f},\\
(\bar{f},\partial_{t}\bar{f})|_{t=0}\in H^{1}\times L^{2}.\end{cases}

(6.23)

Considering
\mathbf{g}^{00}=-1
, let us calculate

\begin{split}-[\square_{\mathbf{g}},\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}]\left<\nabla\right>^{1-r}\bar{f}=&[\mathbf{g}^{\alpha i}-\mathbf{m}^{\alpha i},\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}\partial_{i}]\partial_{\alpha}\left<\nabla\right>^{1-r}\bar{f}\\
&\ +\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}(\partial_{i}\mathbf{g}^{\alpha i}\partial_{\alpha}\left<\nabla\right>^{1-r}\bar{f}).\end{split}

(6.24)

For
r-1\in(0,1)
, by product estimates and a refined Kato-Ponce inequalities (please cite Theorem 1.9 in
2.13
), we have

\begin{split}&\|[\mathbf{g}^{\alpha i}-\mathbf{m}^{\alpha i},\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}\partial_{i}]\partial_{\alpha}\left<\nabla\right>^{1-r}\bar{f}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}(\partial_{i}\mathbf{g}^{\alpha i}\partial_{\alpha}\left<\nabla\right>^{1-r}\bar{f})\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\\
\lesssim&\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|d\bar{f}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|\left<\nabla\right>^{r}(\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{m})\|_{L^{p_{1}}_{x}}\|\left<\nabla\right>^{1-r}d\bar{f}\|_{L^{q_{1}}_{x}},\end{split}

(6.25)

where
p_{1}=\frac{3}{\frac{3}{2}-s+r}
and
q_{1}=\frac{3}{s-r}
. By Sobolev’s inequality, we get

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{r}(\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{m})\|_{L^{p_{1}}_{x}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{m}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}.

(6.26)

By Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality, we can see that
18
18

18

The number
s
and
s_{0}
satisfying
2<s_{0}<s\leq\frac{5}{2}
.

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{1-r}d\bar{f}\|_{L^{q_{1}}_{x}}\lesssim\|d\bar{f}\|^{\frac{s-s_{0}}{\frac{5}{2}-s_{0}}}_{L^{2}_{x}}\|\left<\nabla\right>^{1-s_{0}}d\bar{f}\|^{1-\frac{s-s_{0}}{\frac{5}{2}-s_{0}}}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

(6.27)

Considering
19
19

19

please see the definition of
\mathcal{H}
and (
4.67
)-(
4.69
)

\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{m}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H^{s}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0},

and combining with (
6.24
)-(

), we have

\|[\square_{\mathbf{g}},\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}]\left<\nabla\right>^{1-r}\bar{f}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}(\|d\bar{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}+\|\left<\nabla\right>^{1-s_{0}}d\bar{f}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}).

(6.28)

Using the discussion in case
r=1
, namely (
6.19
), we then get the Strichartz estimates for
\bar{f}
for
\theta<-1
,

\begin{split}\|\left<\nabla\right>^{\theta}d\bar{f}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim&\ \epsilon_{0}(\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}}+\|d\bar{f}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|\left<\nabla\right>^{1-s_{0}}d\bar{f}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}).\end{split}

(6.29)

Note
1-s_{0}<-1
. So we take
\theta=1-s_{0}
, and then we obtain

\begin{split}\|\left<\nabla\right>^{1-s_{0}}d\bar{f}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim&\ \epsilon_{0}(\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}}+\|d\bar{f}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}).\end{split}

(6.30)

Using (
6.30
), (
6.29
) yields

\begin{split}\|\left<\nabla\right>^{\theta}d\bar{f}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim&\ \epsilon_{0}(\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}}+\|d\bar{f}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}),\quad\theta<-1.\end{split}

(6.31)

By (
6.30
), (
6.28
) and the energy estimates for
\bar{f}
, we have

\|d\bar{f}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\|\bar{f}(0)\|_{H^{1}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}\bar{f}(0)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}}.

(6.32)

Substituting
\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}f=\bar{f}
in (
6.31
) and using (
6.32
), we can prove that

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{\theta}d{f}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\ \epsilon_{0}(\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}}),\quad\theta<r-2.

As a result, we also have

\|\left<\nabla\right>^{k}f\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}(\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}}),\quad k<r-1.

Step 4:
The case

. We also set

\begin{split}-[\square_{\mathbf{g}},\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}]\left<\nabla\right>^{1-r}\bar{f}=&[\mathbf{g}^{\alpha i}-\mathbf{m}^{\alpha i},\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}\partial_{i}]\partial_{\alpha}\left<\nabla\right>^{1-r}\bar{f}\\

(6.33)

By Kato-Ponce commutator and product estimates, we have

\begin{split}&\|[\mathbf{g}^{\alpha i}-\mathbf{m}^{\alpha i},\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}\partial_{i}]\partial_{\alpha}\left<\nabla\right>^{1-r}\bar{f}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|\left<\nabla\right>^{r-1}(\partial_{i}\mathbf{g}^{\alpha i}\partial_{\alpha}\left<\nabla\right>^{1-r}\bar{f})\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\\
\lesssim&\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|d\bar{f}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}-\mathbf{m}^{\alpha\beta}\|_{H^{r}_{x}}\|\left<\nabla\right>^{1-r}d\bar{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}},\end{split}

(6.34)

Considering

6.2.
Proof of Proposition
6.1

By Remark
6.1
, we only need to consider the problem if the frequency
\lambda
is large enough, namely

\lambda\geq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}.

In this case, we can construct an approximate solutions to (
4.17
) by using wave packets, and a wave packet has a finer spatial localization than a plane wave solutions. We divide the proof for (
6.2
)-(
6.3
) to two parts. The conclusion (
6.2
) is established in Proposition
6.4
and Proposition
6.5
, and the conclusion (
6.3
) is obtained by Proposition
6.7
.

We introduce a spatially localized mollifier
T_{\lambda}
by

T_{\lambda}f=\chi_{\lambda}*f,\quad\chi_{\lambda}=\lambda^{3}\chi(\lambda^{-1}y),

where
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is supported in the ball
|x|\leq\frac{1}{32}
, and has integral
1
. By choosing
\chi
appropriately, any function
v
with frequency support contained in
\{\xi:|\xi|\leq 4\lambda\}
can be factored
v=T_{\lambda}\widetilde{v}
, where
\|\widetilde{v}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\approx\|v\|_{L^{2}_{x}}
.

Let us now introduce the wave packets which is related to
\mathbf{g}
and the null hypersurfaces
\Sigma
.

Definition 6.1

(
[
50
]
, Definition 8.1)
.

Let
\gamma=\gamma(t)
be a geodesic for
\mathbf{g}
, and let
\Sigma_{\omega,r}
be the null surface introduced in Section
5
that contains
\gamma
, defined by

\Sigma_{\omega,r}=\{(t,x):x_{\omega}-\phi_{\omega,r}(t,x^{\prime}_{\omega})=0\},

where
x_{\omega}=x\cdot\omega
, and
x^{\prime}_{\omega}
are projective coordinates along
\omega
. A normalized wave packet around
\gamma
is a function
f
of the form

f=\epsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lambda^{-1}T_{\lambda}(\varphi\psi),

where

\varphi(t,x)=\delta(x_{\omega}-\phi_{\omega,r}(t,x^{\prime}_{\omega})),\quad\psi=\chi_{0}((\epsilon_{0}\lambda)^{\frac{1}{2}}(x^{\prime}_{\omega}-\gamma_{\omega}(t))).

Here,
\chi_{0}
is a smooth function supported in the set
|x^{\prime}|\leq 1
, with uniform bounds on its derivatives
|\partial^{\alpha}_{x^{\prime}}\chi_{0}(x^{\prime})|\leq c_{\alpha}
.

We give two notations here. We denote
L(\varphi,\psi)
to denote a translation invariant bilinear operator of the form

L(\varphi,\psi)(x)=\int K(y,z)\varphi(x+y)\psi(x+z)dydz,

where
K(y,z)
is a finite measure. If
X
is a Sobolev spaces, we then denote
X_{a}
the same space but with the norm obtained by dimensionless rescaling by
a
,

\|\varphi\|_{X_{a}}=\|\varphi(a\cdot)\|_{X}.

Since
2(s_{0}-1)>2
, then for
a<1
we have
\|\varphi\|_{H^{s_{0}-1}_{a}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{H^{s_{0}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
. In the following, let us introduce what we can get when we take the operator
\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}
on wave packets.

6.2.1.
A normalized wave packet

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
f
be a normalized packet. Then there is another normalized wave packet
\tilde{f}
, and functions
\psi_{m}(t,x^{\prime}_{\omega}),m=0,1,2
, so that

\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}P_{\lambda}f=L(d\mathbf{g},d\tilde{P}_{\lambda}\tilde{f})+\epsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lambda^{-1}P_{\lambda}T_{\lambda}\sum_{m=0,1,2}\psi_{m}\delta^{(m)}(x^{\prime}_{\omega}-\phi_{\omega,r}),

(6.55)

where the functions
\psi_{m}=\psi_{m}(t,x^{\prime}_{\omega})
satisfy the scaled Sobolev estimates

\|\psi_{m}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}H^{s_{0}-1}_{a,x^{\prime}_{\omega}}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}\lambda^{1-m},\quad m=0,1,2,\quad a=(\epsilon_{0}\lambda)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.56)

Proof.

For brevity, we consider the case
\omega=(0,0,1)
. Then
x_{\omega}=x_{3}
, and
x^{\prime}_{\omega}=x^{\prime}
. We write

\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}P_{\lambda}f=\lambda^{-1}([\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}},P_{\lambda}T_{\lambda}]+P_{\lambda}T_{\lambda}\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}})(\varphi\psi).

(6.57)

For the first term in (
6.57
), noting
\mathbf{g}\lambda
supported at frequency
\leq\frac{\lambda}{8}
, then we can write

[\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}},P_{\lambda}T_{\lambda}]=[\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}},P_{\lambda}T_{\lambda}]\tilde{P}_{\lambda}\tilde{T}_{\lambda}

for some multipliers
\tilde{P}_{\lambda},\tilde{T}_{\lambda}
which have the same properties as
P_{\lambda},T_{\lambda}
. Therefore, by using the kernal bounds for
P_{\lambda}T_{\lambda}
, we conclude that

[\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}},P_{\lambda}T_{\lambda}]f=L(d\mathbf{g},df).

For the second term in (
6.57
), we use the Leibniz rule

\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}(\varphi\psi)=\psi\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}\varphi+(\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}_{\lambda}+\mathbf{g}^{\beta\alpha}_{\lambda})\partial_{\alpha}\varphi\partial_{\beta}\psi+\varphi\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}\psi.

(6.58)

We let
\nu
denote the conormal vector field along
\Sigma
,
\nu=dx_{3}-d\phi(t,x^{\prime})
. In the following, we take the greek indices
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 2
.

For the first term in (
6.58
), we can calculate it by

\begin{split}\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha\beta}\varphi=&\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\alpha\beta}(t,x^{\prime},\phi)\nu_{\alpha}\nu_{\beta}\delta^{(2)}_{x_{3}-\phi}-2(\partial_{3}\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\alpha\beta})(t,x^{\prime},\phi)\nu_{\alpha}\nu_{\beta}\delta^{(1)}_{x_{3}-\phi}\\
&+(\partial^{2}_{3}\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\alpha\beta})(t,x^{\prime},\phi)\nu_{\alpha}\nu_{\beta}\delta^{(0)}_{x_{3}-\phi}-\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\alpha\beta})(t,x^{\prime},\phi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}\phi\delta^{(1)}_{x_{3}-\phi}\\
&+\partial_{3}\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\alpha\beta})(t,x^{\prime},\phi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}\phi\delta^{(0)}_{x_{3}-\phi}.\end{split}

Here,
\delta^{(m)}_{x_{3}-\phi}=(\partial^{m}\delta)(x_{3}-\phi)
. By Leibniz rule, we can take

\begin{split}\psi_{0}&=\psi\big\{(\partial^{2}_{3}\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\alpha\beta})(t,x^{\prime},\phi)\nu_{\alpha}\nu_{\beta}+(\partial_{3}\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\alpha\beta})(t,x^{\prime},\phi)\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\phi\big\},\\
\psi_{1}&=\psi\big\{2(\partial_{3}\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\alpha\beta})(t,x^{\prime},\phi)\nu_{\alpha}\nu_{\beta}-\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\alpha\beta}(t,x^{\prime},\phi)\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\phi\big\},\\
\psi_{2}&=\psi(\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}^{\alpha\beta}-\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta})\nu_{\alpha}\nu_{\beta},\end{split}

Due to (
5.62
), Proposition
5.6
, and Corollary
5.4
, we can conclude that this settings of
\psi_{0},\psi_{1},
and
\psi_{2}
satisfy (
6.56
). For the second term in (
6.57
), we have

\begin{split}(\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}_{\lambda}+\mathbf{g}^{\beta\alpha}_{\lambda})\partial_{\alpha}\varphi\partial_{\beta}\psi=&\frac{1}{2}\nu_{\alpha}(\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}_{\lambda}+\mathbf{g}^{\beta\alpha}_{\lambda})(t,x^{\prime},\phi)\partial_{\beta}\psi\delta^{(1)}_{x_{3}-\phi}\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\nu_{\alpha}\partial_{3}(\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}_{\lambda}+\mathbf{g}^{\beta\alpha}_{\lambda})(t,x^{\prime},\phi)\partial_{\beta}\psi\delta^{(0)}_{x_{3}-\phi}.\end{split}

We therefore take

\psi_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\nu_{\alpha}\partial_{3}(\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}_{\lambda}+\mathbf{g}^{\beta\alpha}_{\lambda})(t,x^{\prime},\phi)\partial_{\beta}\psi,\quad\psi_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\nu_{\alpha}(\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}_{\lambda}+\mathbf{g}^{\beta\alpha}_{\lambda})(t,x^{\prime},\phi)\partial_{\beta}\psi.

Since (
5.62
), Proposition
5.6
, and Corollary
5.4
, we can conclude that this settings of
\psi_{0}
and
\psi_{1}
satisfy the estimates (
6.56
).
For the third term in (
6.57
), we can take

\psi_{0}=\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}_{\lambda}(t,x^{\prime},\phi)\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\psi.

Due to (
5.62
), Proposition
5.6
, and Corollary
5.4
, we can conclude that this settings of
\psi_{0}
satisfies the estimates (
6.56
). So we have finished the proof of Proposition
6.2
.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

Seeing (
6.55
)-(
6.56
), a single wave packet is not sufficient for us to constructe approximate solutions to a linear wave equation.

We next consider a superposition of these single wave packets.

6.2.2.
Superpositions of wave packets

Firstly, let us introduce some notations. The index
\omega
stands for the initial orientation of the wave packet at
t=-2
, which varies over a maximal collection of approximately
\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\lambda
unit vectors separated by at least
\epsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. For each
\omega
, we have the orthonormal coordinate system
(x_{\omega},x^{\prime}_{\omega})
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, where
x_{\omega}=x\cdot\omega
, and
x^{\prime}_{\omega}
are projective along
\omega
.

We denote
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by a parallel tiling of rectangles, with length
(8\lambda)^{-1}
in the
x_{\omega}
direction, and
(4\epsilon_{0}\lambda)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
in the other directions
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\omega}
. The index
j
corresponds to a counting of the rectangles in this decomposition. Let
R_{\omega,j}
denote the collection of the doubles of these rectangles, and
\Sigma_{\omega,j}
denote the element of the
\Sigma_{\omega}
(
\Sigma_{\omega}=\cup_{r}\Sigma_{\omega,r}
) foliation upon which
R_{\omega,j}
is centered. Let
\gamma_{\omega,j}
denote the null geodesic contained in
\Sigma_{\omega,j}
which passes through the center of
R_{\omega,j}
at time
t=-2
.

We let
T_{\omega,j}
be the set

T_{\omega,j}=\Sigma_{\omega,j}\cap\{|x^{\prime}_{\omega}-\gamma_{\omega,j}|\leq(\epsilon_{0}\lambda)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\}.

(6.59)

By (
5.64
) and (
5.72
), then the estimate

|dr_{\theta}-(\theta\cdot dx-dt)|\lesssim\epsilon_{1},

holds pointwise uniformly on
[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. This also implies that

|\phi_{\theta,r}(t,x^{\prime}_{\theta})-\phi_{\theta,r^{\prime}}(t,x^{\prime}_{\theta})-(r-r^{\prime})|\lesssim\epsilon_{1}|r-r^{\prime}|.

(6.60)

For simplicity, we set

{\rho}(t)=\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{C^{\delta}_{x}}.

Then (
5.63
) tells us

\|d^{2}_{x^{\prime}_{\omega}}\phi_{\omega,r}(t,x^{\prime}_{\omega})-d^{2}_{x^{\prime}_{\omega}}\phi_{\omega,r^{\prime}}(t,x^{\prime}_{\omega})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x^{\prime}_{\omega}}}\lesssim\epsilon_{2}+{\rho}(t).

(6.61)

By using (
6.60
) and (
6.61
), we get

\|d_{x^{\prime}_{\omega}}\phi_{\omega,r}(t,x^{\prime}_{\omega})-d_{x^{\prime}_{\omega}}\phi_{\omega,r^{\prime}}(t,x^{\prime}_{\omega})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x^{\prime}_{\omega}}}\lesssim(\epsilon_{2}+{\rho}(t))^{\frac{1}{2}}|r-r^{\prime}|^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.62)

For
dx_{\omega}-d\phi_{\omega,r}
is null and also
d\mathbf{g}\leq{\rho}(t)
, this also implies Hölder-
\frac{1}{2}
bounds on
d\phi_{\omega,r}
. So we suppose that
(t,x)\in\Sigma_{\omega,r}
and
(t,y)\in\Sigma_{\omega,r^{\prime}}
, that
|x^{\prime}_{\omega}-y^{\prime}_{\omega}|\leq 2(\epsilon_{0}\lambda)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, and that
|r-r^{\prime}|\leq 2\lambda^{-1}
. Using (
5.65
), we can obtain

|l_{\omega}(t,x)-l_{\omega}(t,y)|\lesssim\epsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\epsilon_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}{\rho}(t)\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Due to
\dot{\gamma}_{\omega}=l_{\omega}
, and
\|{\rho}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}
, so any geodesic in
\Sigma_{\omega}
which intersects a slab
T_{\omega,j}
should be contained in the similar slab of half the scale.

We are ready to introduce a result about a superposition of wave packets from a certain fixed time.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
0<\mu<\delta
. Let a scalar function
v
be formulated by

v(t,x)=\epsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}P_{\lambda}\sum_{\omega,j}T_{\lambda}(f^{\omega,j}\delta_{x_{\omega}-\phi_{\omega,j}(t,x^{\prime}_{\omega})}).

(6.63)

Set
{\rho}(t)=\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{C^{\delta}_{x}}
and
a=(\epsilon_{0}\lambda)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Then we have

\|{v}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\sum_{\omega,j}\|f^{\omega,j}\|^{2}_{H^{1+\mu}_{a}},\qquad\qquad\ \ \text{if}\ \ {\rho}(t)\leq\epsilon_{0},

(6.64)

and

\|{v}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}^{-1}{\rho}(t)\sum_{\omega,j}\|f^{\omega,j}\|^{2}_{H^{1+\mu}_{a}},\qquad\text{if}\ \ {\rho}(t)\geq\epsilon_{0}.

(6.65)

Proof.

This proof follows ideas of Smith-Tataru
[
50
]
(Lemma 8.5 and Lemma 8.6 on page 340-345). Therefore we omit the details here.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

By (
6.64
) and (
6.65
), we get

\|v(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\left(1+\epsilon_{0}^{-1}{\rho}(t)\right)\sum_{\omega,j}\|f^{\omega,j}\|^{2}_{H^{1+\mu}_{a}}.

(6.66)

Proposition 6.4

(
[
50
]
, Proposition 8.4)
.

Let
f=\sum_{\omega,j}a_{\omega,j}f^{\omega,j}
, where
f^{\omega,j}
are normalized wave packets supported in
T_{\omega,j}
. Then we have

\|dP_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim(\sum_{\omega,j}a^{2}_{\omega,j})^{\frac{1}{2}},

(6.67)

and

\|\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}P_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}(\sum_{\omega,j}a^{2}_{\omega,j})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.68)

Proof.

We first prove a weaker estimate comparing with (
6.67
)

\|dP_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim(\sum_{\omega,j}a^{2}_{\omega,j})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.69)

By using (
6.66
) and replacing
P_{\lambda}
by
\lambda^{-1}\nabla P_{\lambda}
, and
f^{\omega,j}
by
a_{\omega,j}\psi^{\omega,j}
, we have

\|\nabla P_{\lambda}f(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim(1+\epsilon_{0}^{-1}{\rho}(t))\sum_{\omega,j}a^{2}_{\omega,j}.

Due to the fact
\|{\rho}\|_{L^{2}_{t}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}
, we can see that

\|\nabla P_{\lambda}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\sum_{\omega,j}a^{2}_{\omega,j}.

(6.70)

We also need to get the similar estimate for the time derivatives. We can calculate

\partial_{t}\psi=\dot{\gamma}(t)(\epsilon_{0}\lambda)^{\frac{1}{2}}\tilde{\psi},\quad\partial_{t}\delta(x_{\omega}-\phi_{\omega,j})=\partial_{t}\phi_{\omega,j}\delta^{(1)}(x_{\omega}-\phi_{\omega,j}).

For
\dot{\gamma}\in L^{\infty}_{t}
and
\partial_{t}\phi_{\omega,j}\in L^{\infty}_{t}
, then we have

\|\partial_{t}P_{\lambda}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\sum_{\omega,j}a^{2}_{\omega,j}.

(6.71)

Combining (
6.70
) and (
6.71
), we have proved (
6.69
). To prove (
6.68
), we use the formula (
6.55
). Considering the right hand of (
6.55
), by using (
6.69
), we can bound the first term by

\|L(d\mathbf{g},d\tilde{P}_{\lambda}\tilde{f})\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\|d\tilde{P}_{\lambda}\tilde{f}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}(\sum_{\omega,j}a^{2}_{\omega,j})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

It only remains for us to estimate the second right term on (
6.55
). Set

\Phi=\epsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}P_{\lambda}T_{\lambda}\left(\sum_{\omega,j}a_{\omega,j}\cdot\sum_{m=0,1,2}\psi^{\omega,j}_{m}\delta^{(m)}_{x_{\omega}-\phi_{\omega,j}}\right).

By (
6.56
), we then have

\begin{split}\|\Phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}=&(1+{\rho}(t)\epsilon_{0}^{-1})\sum_{\omega,j}a^{2}_{\omega,j}\sum_{m=0,1,2}\lambda^{m-1}\|\psi^{\omega,j}_{m}(t)\|^{2}_{H^{1+\mu}_{a}}\\
\lesssim&(1+{\rho}(t)\epsilon_{0}^{-1})\epsilon_{0}^{2}\sum_{\omega,j}a^{2}_{\omega,j}.\end{split}

So we further get

\begin{split}\|\Phi\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim&\epsilon_{0}(\sum_{\omega,j}a^{2}_{\omega,j})^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int^{2}_{-2}[1+{\rho}(t)\epsilon_{0}^{-1}]^{\frac{1}{2}}dt\right)\\
\lesssim&\epsilon_{0}(\sum_{\omega,j}a^{2}_{\omega,j})^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int^{2}_{-2}[1+{\rho}(t)\epsilon_{0}^{-1}]dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
\lesssim&\epsilon_{0}(\sum_{\omega,j}a^{2}_{\omega,j})^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

Hence, the second right term on (
6.55
) can be estimated by
\epsilon_{0}(\sum_{\omega,j}a^{2}_{\omega,j})^{\frac{1}{2}}
. So we have proved (
6.68
). Using (
6.69
) and (
6.68
), and classical energy estimates for linear wave equation, we obtain (
6.67
).
∎

6.2.3.
Matching the initial data

Although we have constructed the approximate solutions using superpositions of normalized wave packets, we also need to complete this construction, i.e. matching the initial data for this type of solutions. Since the metric
\mathbf{g}
equals to the Minkowski metric for
t\in[-2,-\frac{3}{2}]
, so it’s natural for us to work with wave packets near
t=-2
for the Minkowski wave operator. We refer to the construction from
[
50
, Proposition 8.7, page 346]

or Smith
[
47
, page 815, Section 4]
.

Proposition 6.5

(
[
50
]
, Proposition 8.7)
.

Given any initial data
(f_{0},f_{1})\in H^{1}\times L^{2}
, there exists a function of the form

f=\sum_{\omega,j}a_{\omega,j}f^{\omega,j},

where the function
f^{\omega,j}
are normalized wave packets, such that

f(-2)=P_{\lambda}f_{0},\quad\partial_{t}f(-2)=P_{\lambda}f_{1}.

Furthermore,

\sum_{\omega,j}a_{\omega,j}^{2}\lesssim\|P_{\lambda}f_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\|P_{\lambda}f_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

Remark 6.4
.

The original version of Proposition 8.7 (
[
50
]
, page 346) takes the initial data
(u_{0},u_{1})
. By replacing
(u_{0},u_{1})
in Proposition 8.7 (
[
50
]
, page 346) to
(P_{\lambda}f_{0},P_{\lambda}f_{1})
, our Proposition
6.5
holds.

Remark 6.5
.

Considering the definition of a wave packet, together with the regularity of the
\Sigma_{\omega,r}
, then we can use the quantities
\psi_{\omega,r}
and
\gamma_{\omega,r}
to keep the type of
f
for
t\in[-2,2]
.

If
t_{0}=-2
in Proposition (
6.1
), then the function
f
is the approximate solution matching the initial data by using Proposition
6.5
.

If
t_{0}\in(-2,2)
, then we can use an iteration process as follows. Basically, we hope to find the exact solution
\Phi
to the Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}\Phi=0,\qquad(t,x)\in[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&(\Phi,\partial_{t}\Phi)|_{t=t_{0}}=(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}).\end{cases}

Let
\Phi_{\lambda}=P_{\lambda}\Phi
be the approximate solution with the initial data
(\Phi_{\lambda},\partial_{t}\Phi_{\lambda})|_{t=-2}=({P}_{\lambda}\Phi(-2),{P}_{\lambda}\partial_{t}\Phi(-2))
. Then we have

\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}\Phi_{\lambda}=[\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}},P_{\lambda}]\Phi.

The energy estimates tell us that

\|\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}\Phi_{\lambda}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\|d\mathbf{g}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\|d\Phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}(\|\Phi_{0}\|_{H^{1}}+\|\Phi_{1}\|_{L^{2}}).

However, it does not match the data at
t=t_{0}
. Fortunately, we can obtain

\begin{split}&P_{\lambda}\Phi_{0}-\Phi_{\lambda}(t_{0})=P_{\lambda}\Phi_{0}^{1},\qquad\Phi_{0}^{1}=P_{\lambda}\Phi_{0}-\Phi_{\lambda}(t_{0}),\\
&P_{\lambda}\Phi_{1}-\partial_{t}\Phi_{\lambda}(t_{0})=P_{\lambda}\Phi_{1}^{1},\ \quad\Phi_{1}^{1}=P_{\lambda}\Phi_{1}-\partial_{t}\Phi_{\lambda}(t_{0}).\end{split}

By energy estimates and commutator estimates, we have

\begin{split}\|\Phi_{0}^{1}\|_{H^{1}}+\|\Phi_{1}^{1}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim&\|\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}(P_{\lambda}\Phi-\Phi)\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\\
\lesssim&\|\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}}\Phi_{\lambda}\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}+\|[\square_{\mathbf{g}_{\lambda}},\Phi_{\lambda}]\Phi\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}L^{2}_{x}}\\
\lesssim&\epsilon_{0}(\|\Phi_{0}\|_{H^{1}}+\|\Phi_{1}\|_{L^{2}}).\end{split}

Since the norm of the error is much smaller than the initial size of the data,
we can repeat this process with data
(\Phi_{0}^{1},\Phi_{1}^{1})
, and sum this series to
obtain a smooth function
\Phi_{\lambda}
with data
(P_{\lambda}\Phi_{0},P_{\lambda}\Phi_{1})
at time
t=t_{0}
. As a result, it can also match the given data at time
t=t_{0}
.

6.2.4.
Overlap estimates

Since the foliations
\Sigma_{\omega,r}
varing with
\omega
and
r
, so a fixed
\Sigma_{\omega,r}
may intersect with other
\Sigma_{\omega^{\prime},r^{\prime}}
. As a result, we should be clear about the number of
\lambda
-slabs which contain two given points in the space-time
[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Corollary 6.6

(
[
50
]
, Proposition 9.2)
.

For all points
P_{1}=(t_{1},x_{1})
and
P_{2}=(t_{2},x_{2})
in space-time
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and
\epsilon_{0}\lambda\geq 1
, the number
N_{\lambda}(P_{1},P_{2})
of slabs of scale
\lambda
that contain both
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
satisfies the bound

\begin{split}N_{\lambda}(P_{1},P_{2})\lesssim&\epsilon_{0}^{-1}|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{-1}.\end{split}

Remark 6.6
.

Taking
n=3
for Proposition 9.2 in
[
50
]
, we then obtain Corollary
6.6
.

6.2.5.
The proof of Estimate (
6.3
)

After the construction of approximate solutions, we still need to prove the key estimate (
6.3
). This can be directly derived by the following result. Before that, let us define
\mathcal{T}=\cup_{\omega,j}T_{\omega,j}
, where
T_{\omega,j}
is set in (
6.59
). We also denote
\chi_{{J}}
be a smooth cut-off function, and
\chi_{J}=1
on a set
J
.

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
t\in[-2,2]
and

f={\sum}_{J\in\mathcal{T}}a_{J}\chi_{J}f_{J},

where
\sum_{J\in\mathcal{T}}a_{J}^{2}\leq 1
and
f_{J}
are normalized wave packets in
J
. Then
24
24

24

We need
r>1
in (
6.3
) for there is a factor
(\ln\lambda)^{\frac{1}{2}}
in (
6.72
).

\|f\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\ln\lambda)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.72)

Proof.

This proof also follows Smith-Tataru
[
50
]
(Proposition 10.1 on page 353). Here we use slightly different factors in (
6.72
).

Let us first make a partition of the time-interval
[-2,2]
. By decomposition, there exists a partition
\left\{I_{j}\right\}
of the interval
[-2,2]
into disjoint subintervals
I_{j}
such that with the size of each
I_{j}
,
|I_{j}|\simeq\lambda^{-1}
, and the number of subintervals
j\simeq\lambda
. By mean value theorem, there exists a number
t_{j}
such that

\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq\textstyle{\sum}_{j}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{I_{j}}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq\textstyle{\sum}_{j}\|f(t_{j},\cdot)\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lambda^{-1},

(6.73)

where
t_{j}
is located in
I_{j}
, and
|t_{j+1}-t_{j}|\simeq\lambda^{-1}
. Let us explain the (
6.73
) as follows. For simplicity, let
I_{j_{0}}=[0,\lambda^{-1}]
and
I_{j_{0}+1}=[\lambda^{-1},2\lambda^{-1}]
. By mean value theorem, on
I_{j_{0}}
, we have

\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{I_{j_{0}}}L^{\infty}_{x}}=\|f(t_{j_{0}},\cdot)\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lambda^{-1}.

We also have

\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{I_{j_{0}+1}}L^{\infty}_{x}}=\|f(t_{j_{0}+1},\cdot)\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lambda^{-1}.

If
t_{j_{0}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{-1}
or
t_{j_{0}+1}\geq\frac{3}{2}\lambda^{-1}
, then
|t_{j_{0}+1}-t_{j_{0}}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{-1}
. Otherwise,
t_{j_{0}}\in[\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{-1},\lambda^{-1}]
and
t_{j_{0}+1}\in[\lambda^{-1},\frac{3}{2}\lambda^{-1}]
. In this case,
we combine
I_{j_{0}},I_{j_{0}+1}
together and set
I^{*}_{j_{0}}=I_{j_{0}}\cup I_{j_{0}+1}
. On the new interval
I^{*}_{j_{0}}
, we can see that

\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{I^{*}_{j_{0}}}L^{\infty}_{x}}=2\|f(t^{*}_{j_{0}},\cdot)\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lambda^{-1},

and

2\|f(t^{*}_{j_{0}},\cdot)\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}=\|f(t_{j_{0}},\cdot)\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|f(t_{j_{0}+1},\cdot)\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}.

Since
f
is a contituous function, we have

\|f(t^{*}_{j_{0}},\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}=\left\{\frac{1}{2}\big(\|f(t_{j_{0}},\cdot)\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|f(t_{j_{0}+1},\cdot)\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big)\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}},\qquad t^{*}_{j_{0}}\in[\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{-1},\frac{3}{2}\lambda^{-1}].

In case
t^{*}_{j_{0}}\in[\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{-1},\frac{3}{2}\lambda^{-1}]
, we set
t_{j_{0}}:=t^{*}_{j_{0}}
. On the next time-interval
I_{j_{0}+2}=[2\lambda^{-1},3\lambda^{-1}]
, we obtain

\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{I_{j_{0}+2}}L^{\infty}_{x}}=\|f(t_{j_{0}+1},\cdot)\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lambda^{-1},\quad t_{j_{0}+1}\in[2\lambda^{-1},3\lambda^{-1}].

Hence, we have proved
|t_{j_{0}+1}-t_{j_{0}}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{-1}
. So we can use this way to decompose
[-2,2]
. Therefore, to prove (
6.72
), and taking (
6.73
) into account, we only need to show that

\textstyle{\sum}_{j}|f(t_{j},x_{j})|^{2}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\lambda\ln\lambda,

(6.74)

where
x_{j}
is arbitrarily chosen. We then set the points
P_{j}=(t_{j},x_{j})
.

Since each points lies in at most
\approx\epsilon_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}
slabs, so we may assume that
|a_{J}|\geq\epsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Then we decompose the sum
f=\sum_{J\in\mathcal{T}}a_{J}\chi_{J}f_{J}
dyadically with respect to the size of
a_{J}
. We next decompose the sum over
j
via a dyadic decomposition in the numbers of slabs containing
(t_{j},x_{j})
. We may assume that we are summing over
M
points
25
25

25

We also remark that
M
is
\simeq\lambda
, for
t_{j}\in[-2,2]
and
|t_{j+1}-t_{j}|\simeq\lambda^{-1}
.

(t_{j},x_{j})
, each of which is contained in approximately
L
slabs
26
26

26

So
L\lesssim\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\lambda
by using Corollary
6.6
.
. Then
|f(t_{j},x_{j})|\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}}L
and

\textstyle{\sum}_{j}|f(t_{j},\mathbf{x}_{j})|^{2}\lesssim L^{2}MN^{-1}.

(6.75)

Combining (
6.74
) with (
6.75
), we only need to prove

L^{2}MN^{-1}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\lambda\ln\lambda.

(6.76)

This is a counting problem, and we shall prove (
6.76
) by calculating in two different ways. First consider the number
K
of pairs
(i,j)
for which
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
are contained in a common slab, counted with multiplicity. For
J\in\mathcal{T}
, we denote by
n_{J}
the number of points
P_{j}
contained
27
27

27

For
J
, if it contains
3
points
P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}
, then we will count it
(P_{1},P_{2}),(P_{2},P_{1}),(P_{1},P_{3}),(P_{3},P_{1}),(P_{2},P_{3}),(P_{3},P_{2})
in
K
. Therefore it’s
3^{2}
.
in
J
. Then

K=\sum_{n_{J}\geq 2}n_{J}^{2}.

By Jensen’s inequality, we have

K=\sum_{n_{J}\geq 2}n_{J}^{2}\gtrsim N^{-1}(\sum_{n_{J}\geq 2}n_{J})^{2}.

Note that
\sum_{J\in\mathcal{T}}n_{J}\approx ML
. We consider it into two cases. If

\sum_{n_{J}\geq 2}n_{J}\leq\sum_{n_{J}=1}n_{J},

then
N\approx ML
. In this case, combining with the fact that
L\lesssim\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\lambda
, then (
6.76
) holds. Otherwise, we have

K\gtrsim N^{-1}M^{2}L^{2}.

(6.77)

In this case, by using Corollary
6.6
, we obtain

K\lesssim\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq M,i\neq j}|t_{i}-t_{j}|^{-1}.

(6.78)

The sum is maximized in the case that
t_{j}
are close as possible, i.e. if the
t_{j}
are consecutive multiples of
\lambda^{-1}
. Therefore, (
6.78
) yields

K\lesssim\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\lambda\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq M,i\neq j}|i-j|^{-1}\lesssim M\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\lambda\ln\lambda,

(6.79)

where we use the fact that
M\simeq\lambda
. Combining (
6.79
) and (
6.77
), we get (
6.76
). So we have finished the proof of Proposition
6.7
.
∎

7.
Continuous dependence

In this part, we discuss the continuous dependence of solutions by using Tao’s frequency envelope approach
[
59
]
. The approach was used in wave maps
[
59
,
60
]
, incompressible Euler equations
[
58
]
, quasilinear hyperbolic system
[
24
]
, and compressible Euler equations
[
8
,
63
]
. We should also mention that there are more classical methods for continuous dependence goes back to
[
28
,
9
,
6
,
7
]
.

Corollary 7.1

(Continuous dependence)
.

Assume
2<s_{0}<s\leq\frac{5}{2}
. Let the initial data
(\mathbf{u}_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
be stated in Theorem
1.2
. Then

\|u_{0}^{0}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|h_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\|_{H^{s_{0}}}\leq M_{0}.

(7.1)

Let
\{(\mathbf{u}_{0m},h_{0m},\mathbf{w}_{0m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}
be a sequence of initial data converging to
(\mathbf{u}_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
in space
H^{s}\times H^{s}\times H^{s_{0}}
, where
\mathbf{w}_{0m}=\mathrm{vort}(\mathrm{e}^{h_{0m}}\mathbf{u}_{0m})
. Then for every
m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, there exists
T>0
such that the Cauchy problem (
1.20
) with the initial data
(\mathbf{u}_{0m},h_{0m},\mathbf{w}_{0m})
has a unique solution
(\mathbf{u}_{m},h_{m},\mathbf{w}_{m})
on
[0,T]
, and the Cauchy problem (
1.20
) with the initial data
(\mathbf{u}_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
has a unique solution
(\mathbf{u},h,\mathbf{w})
on
[0,T]
. Moreover, the sequence
\{(\mathbf{u}_{m},h_{m},\mathbf{w}_{m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}
converges to
(\mathbf{u},h,\mathbf{w})
on
[0,T]
in
H^{s}_{x}\times H_{x}^{s}\times H_{x}^{s_{0}}
.

Proof.

For
\{(\mathbf{u}_{0m},h_{0m},\mathbf{w}_{0m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}
is a sequence which converges to
(\mathbf{u}_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
in space
H^{s}\times H^{s}\times H^{s_{0}}
, by using (
7.1
), we obtain

\|u^{0}_{0m}-1,\mathring{\mathbf{u}}_{0m}\|_{H^{s}}+\|h_{0m}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{0m}\|_{H^{s_{0}}}\leq 100M_{0},\quad\text{for \ large}\ m,

By using the existence and uniqueness result in Theorem
1.2
and Corollary
3.5
, there exists
T>0
such that the Cauchy problem (
1.20
) with the initial data
(\mathbf{u}_{0m},h_{0m},\mathbf{w}_{0m})
has a unique solution
(\mathbf{u}_{m},h_{m},\mathbf{w}_{m})
on
[0,T]
, and the Cauchy problem (
1.20
) with the initial data
(\mathbf{u}_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
has a unique solution
(\mathbf{u},h,\mathbf{w})
on
[0,T]
. Next, we divide the proof of convergence into three steps.

Step 1: Convergence in weaker spaces
. By the
L^{2}_{x}
energy estimates, and using the Strichartz estimate (
1.40
), for
m,l\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, we have

\begin{split}\|(\mathbf{u}_{m}-\mathbf{u}_{l},h_{m}-h_{l},\mathbf{w}_{m}-\mathbf{w}_{l})\|_{L_{x}^{2}}\lesssim\|(\mathbf{u}_{0m}-\mathbf{u}_{0l},h_{0m}-h_{0l},\mathbf{w}_{0m}-\mathbf{w}_{0l})\|_{H^{s}\times H^{s}\times H^{s_{0}}}.\end{split}

As a result, for
t\in[0,T]
, we have

\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}(\mathbf{u}_{m},h_{m},\mathbf{w}_{m})=(\mathbf{u},h,\mathbf{w})\quad\mathrm{in}\ \ L^{2}_{x}.

Due to the interpolation formula, it follows

\|\mathbf{u}_{m}-\mathbf{u}\|_{H_{x}^{\sigma}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{u}_{m}-\mathbf{u}\|^{1-\frac{\sigma}{s}}_{L_{x}^{2}}\|\mathbf{u}_{m}-\mathbf{u}\|^{\frac{\sigma}{s}}_{H_{x}^{s}},\quad 0\leq\sigma<s.

This leads to

\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{u}_{m}=\mathbf{u}\ \ \mathrm{in}\ \ H^{\sigma}_{x},\ \ 0\leq\sigma<s,

In a similar way, we can obtain
